11	## For every proposal
12	Read the question's stimulus, stem, all five choices, the key, and the old and new text in full. Then decide:
13	- **approve** — the new text is correct: every quote matches the choice or stimulus exactly, the logic is right for this question and key, the verdict for the letter agrees with the key (correct letter is argued as correct, wrong letters as wrong), nothing outside the defect was damaged, and it reads clearly.
14	- **fix** — the edit is the right idea but has a mistake you can correct. Give the full corrected `new`.
15	- **reject** — the edit is wrong, unnecessary, or makes things worse. Say why.
16	
17	Hard rules for approve/fix: no added em dashes; no added gendered pronouns (he, she, his, her...) for people in the stimulus; no mention of Logic Games; never change which choice is credited; a wrong-answer note must say why that choice is wrong, a correct-answer note why it is right.
18

20

22	- **Weak**: complete or nearly so, but loose: role labels as notes ("This is a premise"), "not relevant" notes, no prediction. A student gets little from it.

1	# Step 2 brief: propose exact fixes for defective LR explanations
2	
3	You are fixing **real errors** in LSAT U's LR Question Explanations, the teaching students read after answering. You do NOT write to any database. You produce a proposal file. Another reviewer checks every proposal against the question before anything is applied, so precision matters more than volume.
4	
5	## Read-only sources (local mirror of the live database, refreshed 2026-10-02/03)
6	Directory: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/`
7	- `lr_questions.json` — id, qid (e.g. PT146-S3-Q1), stimulus, prompt (the stem), correct_choice, question_type
8	- `lr_answer_choices.json` — question_id, letter, choice_text, tag, is_correct
9	- `lr_explanations.json` — id, question_id, style (`standard`, `brief`, `another_way`, `single`)
10	- `lr_explanation_steps.json` — id, explanation_id, position, label, body (the Argument Breakdown steps)
11	- `lr_explanation_choice_notes.json` — id, explanation_id, letter, body (the Choice Notes)
12	
13	Students see the first style present in the order standard, brief, another_way, single, and the selector lets them see the others. Fix the defect in **every style where it occurs**, not just one.
14	
15	Also available, the question bank students are graded on: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu/public/data/PT{n}-S{s}-LR.json` (fields questionNumber, stimulus, questionStem, answerChoices, correctAnswer). When the DB and bank wording differ, the bank is what students read.
16	
17	Load the JSON with Python (`python -I`), never by reading the whole file into your context. Write helper scripts to your own scratch directory.
18	
19	## What to do for each item on your list
20	1. Find the question and the row(s) the item describes. Read the stimulus, the stem, all five choices, the key, and the full explanation (all styles).
21	2. Confirm the defect is real. The list was written by earlier readers and may be wrong or already fixed. If it is not real, record a `skip` with the reason.
22	3. Write the smallest edit that makes the text correct:
23	   - Keep the author's voice, structure and wording everywhere outside the defect.
24	   - A misquote, slip or wrong-letter reference: correct the words.
25	   - Garbled or flattened transcription (scrambled diagrams, run-together words, truncation): reconstruct the intended text from the stimulus and the surrounding explanation. Put each diagram chain on its own line, with `→` arrows. If you cannot reconstruct it with confidence, rewrite only that sentence or chain plainly and correctly.
26	   - A note that never says why its choice is wrong or right (verdict-only, pasted from another letter, identical to another note, empty, placeholder): write a real note of 1–3 sentences that states exactly why, tied to the choice's own words and the stimulus.
27	   - Overbroad rules stated as fact ("this answer is always wrong"), mocking or political asides, Logic Games analogies, claims about answer placement: remove or soften them so the teaching point stands. There are no Logic Games on the current LSAT; never mention them.
28	   - Never change which choice is credited. If you believe the key itself is wrong, record a `flag`, not an edit.
29	4. New or rewritten text rules: no em dashes (use a comma, colon, or a new sentence); no gendered pronouns for people in the stimulus (use the name, "the author", "they"); plain, direct sentences; teach the reasoning, do not just assert it; no filler ("Great question", "it's important to note").
30	5. Double-check every proposal: re-read the edited text next to the choice text and stimulus, and confirm the logic and every quote are exact.
31	
32	## Output
33	Write JSON Lines to the output path you were given. One object per proposed row edit:
34	```json
35	{"item": "short quote of the list item", "qid": "PT146-S3-Q1", "table": "lr_explanation_choice_notes", "row_id": "<uuid>", "column": "body", "style": "standard", "letter": "A", "old": "<exact current full body>", "new": "<full corrected body>", "kind": "slip|misquote|transcription|verdict-only|register|empty|wrong-question", "why": "one sentence: what was wrong and how the new text is correct", "evidence": "the exact stimulus/choice words that prove it"}
36	```
37	`old` must be the exact full current value of that column, copied from the mirror. `new` is the full replacement value.
38	
39	For items you do not fix, write one line: `{"item": "...", "qid": "...", "skip": "reason"}` or `{"item": "...", "qid": "...", "flag": "suspected mis-key or needs a source: reason"}`.
40	
41	Allowed tables and columns: `lr_explanation_steps.body`, `lr_explanation_steps.label`, `lr_explanation_choice_notes.body`. Do not propose edits to lr_questions or lr_answer_choices; if the problem is in the question or choice text itself, use `flag`.

43	When finished, reply with: counts of edits, skips and flags; and any item you are unsure of, with the reason.

8	# LR golden set: the 51 explanations new ones are modelled on
9	
10	The model set for writing the 772 missing LR explanations (plan: [[../../brainstorms/2026-09-26-lr-explanation-coverage]]). Every pick is an existing LSAT Lab explanation in the LSAT U database (the database, not the local markdown, is the source of truth), read in full and scored against a rubric built by comparing strong and loose explanations. They are references, not edits: nothing here changes an existing explanation.
11	
12	- Full rubric, strong-vs-loose comparison, step structure per type, voice notes: [[golden-set-observations]]
13	- All 80 candidates, scores and the family x feature coverage grid: [[golden-set-candidates]]

15	- Nearest-neighbor scan: `system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py` ranks every complete DB explanation by type, shared features, stimulus shape and quality for a given question; `show.py SLOT ...` prints one in full. Each draft names the neighbors it was modelled on.
16	
17	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
18	
19	Length does not. These do:
20	
21	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
22	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
23	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
24	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
25	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
26	
27	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.
28	
29	## Do not copy from the corpus
30	
31	Joshua, 2026-09-26: a personal jab at the test is fine; it makes the voice personal.

33

35	- Typos and slips carried in the source (flagged in the table).

===== PT158-S2-Q2 Flaw key A
STIM: Council president: Councilmember Smith has proposed a new city ordinance prohibiting the use of the "bait-and-switch" sales tactic. Smith must have amnesia. Four years ago he owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch. Clearly, Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
STEM: The council president's reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the president
## Conclusion (Clearly)
Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
## Evidence
He's currently proposing a new city ordinance prohibiting "bait-and-switch", but four years ago he owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch.
## Evaluate
Whenever we're doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we consider the 2 of the 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals: Ad Hominem - This refers to when an author dismisses an argument because the source of the argument has a biased interest or conflicting past behavior. Unproven vs. Proven False - This refers to when an author argues that "because there's no evidence for X, or because someone's attempt to prove X failed, that shows that X is false". This looks to be an example of Ad Hominem. The author isn't saying that Smith's claim is false, so it couldn't be Unproven vs. Proven False. She's just dismissing his view, saying "it doesn't even merit consideration". And why? Because of Smith's conflicting past behavior. We should never reject someone's suggestion that we do Y just because that person used to say/do the opposite of Y. People and circumstances can change. Maybe there was someone who was really into cryptocurrency when it first came out but now is against it because of how bad its energy demands are for the environment. We can't say, "Don't listen to that crypto-critic. After all, he was way into Bitcoin when it first came out."
## Goal
Look for an answer describing this famous flaw; often it sounds like "the author dismisses an argument because of its source rather than its content". Otherwise, it might be a more specific answer that says, "because Person X did thing A in the past, we shouldn't listen to X's idea to do away with thing A."
-- A: This answer precisely identifies the argument's flaw. The council president dismisses the proposed ordinance entirely because of who proposed it — Smith, a former bait-and-switch practitioner — rather than because of any problem with the ordinance itself. The merits of a proposal are logically independent of the personal history of its author. Smith's past use of bait-and-switch might make him a hypocrite, but it says nothing about whether the ordinance is well-crafted, enforceable, or beneficial to consumers. By attacking the source rather than the content, the president avoids engaging with the substance of the proposal entirely. This is a classic ad hominem fallacy: the argument treats biographical information about the proposer as grounds for rejecting the proposition.
-- B: This answer describes using a single incompatible fact to dismiss a claim as false. But the argument does not work that way. The president is not identifying a fact that contradicts the ordinance and using it to prove the ordinance is wrong. Instead, the president is using a fact about Smith's personal history to dismiss the ordinance without evaluating it. The distinction matters: the incompatibility described in this answer would be between evidence and the claim being evaluated, but here the "evidence" (Smith's history) has no logical bearing on the claim (the ordinance's merit).

Even if an argument did use a single incompatible fact to reject a claim, that is not inherently fallacious. A single counterexample can legitimately refute a universal claim. The flaw described here — relying on one piece of contradictory evidence — is a valid form of reasoning in many contexts, not a logical error. The actual flaw in the argument is ad hominem reasoning, not insufficient evidence against the proposal.
-- C: This answer suggests the argument fails to distinguish between deceptive and legitimate sales tactics. While such a distinction could theoretically be relevant to a discussion about regulating sales practices, it does not describe the actual flaw in this argument. The president never discusses the substance of sales tactics at all — the president never addresses what the ordinance would prohibit or whether bait-and-switch is appropriately classified. The entire argument operates at the level of "who proposed it" rather than "what it proposes." The fundamental flaw is the ad hominem attack on Smith, not a failure to make nuanced distinctions about different types of sales tactics.
-- D: Circular reasoning occurs when the conclusion simply restates one of its own premises — when the argument assumes what it is trying to prove. For this to apply, the president's conclusion (the ordinance does not merit consideration) would need to be the same as one of the premises. But the premise is that Smith used bait-and-switch four years ago, and the conclusion is that his ordinance should be dismissed. These are different claims. The argument may be invalid (because the premise is irrelevant to the conclusion), but it is not circular — it does not assume its own conclusion. Circular arguments at least have the right kind of connection between premise and conclusion; this argument's problem is the wrong kind of connection entirely.
-- E: A sampling or generalization flaw occurs when a conclusion about a broader category is drawn from an unrepresentative or insufficient sample. The president is not generalizing from instances at all. The argument does not say "Smith's store used bait-and-switch and was bad, therefore all bait-and-switch is bad" or "one case proves a pattern." The president's reasoning is entirely about Smith as an individual and his relationship to the ordinance he proposed. The flaw is about the type of evidence used (biographical rather than substantive), not about the quantity of instances examined. No sampling, surveying, or generalizing is involved in the argument.

ls: cannot access '../ai-os-projects/lsatu/wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md': No such file or directory
# Copy corrections

Every student-facing wording fix, with the reason it was wrong. Read this before
writing or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.

Companion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This
says what has actually gone wrong and why.

Started 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a
correction is made.

## The rules these corrections came from

Three rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was
enforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface
added later starts uncovered.

- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.
- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`
  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.
- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.

Guards now live with the content rather than with each component:
`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every
surface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.

## Corrections

### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)

Joshua's marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing

2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same r

A rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equiva

### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)

Joshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: "Read each whole idea," "Find what the statements guarantee," the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goa

You write NOTHING to any database. You produce a proposal file; an independent verifier checks every item before anything is applied. Precision beats volume.\n\n## Your inputs\nBatch file: a JSON list of qids. Per qid, a packet at `PACKETS/<qid>.json` (load with `python -I`, never read big files wholesale). A packet has: the question (stimulus, stem, five choices, key; `bank` is what students actually read, so quote the bank wording when it differs from the DB), the current explanation (`steps` with position/label/body/row_id; `notes` with letter/body/row_id), and `known_problem` (a reader's note on what is wrong; it may be stale or wrong).\n\nRead the standard before writing:\n- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/golden-set.md` (what top-tier looks like) and `golden-set-observations.md` (step structure per question type, rubric).\n- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/teaching-voice.md`, `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md` (voice), `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md`.\n- Study 2 to 3 strong explanations of the same type: `python -I C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lr-explanations/show.py PT146-S1-Q21` prints one in full (use golden-set qids of your question's family). Model the structure, never copy sentences or examples. Two golden picks (PT145-S2-Q23, PT138-S2-Q25) are reference-only because their logic is wrong; do not use them.\n\n## For each qid\n1. Read the stimulus, stem, all five choices and the key from the packet. Work out the correct answer yourself first. If you disagree with the key, do not rewrite: output a `flag`.\n2. Read the current explanation. Decide:\n   - `rewrite`: it is weak, thin, wrongly registered, wrong, or missing parts.\n   - `skip`: it is already good (record why in one sentence). Do not churn good text.\n3. A rewrite keeps the existing step structure and reuses each existing row. For every existing step keep its `row_id`, and set `body` (and `label` if needed). Keep a step's body verbatim when it is already sound; improve only what is weak. Do not drop any existing step; if one is junk, rewrite it into something useful. You may add steps (row_id null). If the explanation has no breakdown steps, create the standard set for the type (usually Conclusion, Evidence, Evaluate, Goal; see golden-set-observations for other types).\n4. Choice notes: exactly five, letters A to E, each reusing its `row_id` (null if the letter has no row). Standards:\n   - Correct-answer note: teaches the move so a student can repeat it on the next question (what in the choice matches the prediction, what it does with the stimulus's words).\n   - Each wrong-answer note: says exactly why it fails, tied to the choice's own words and the stimulus, and usually gives a test or counterexample. 1 to 3 sentences, about 20 to 60 words. The most tempting trap may run longer.\n   - Never a bare verdict (\"This is irrelevant\"). Never a role label as the whole note. Never restate the choice and stop.\n5. Re-read every quote against the choice or stimulus: exact match to the bank wording. Check every logical claim, especially conditionals (sufficient vs necessary, contrapositive, negation).\n\n## Writing rules (hard)\n- No em dashes or en dashes used as dashes. Use a comma, colon, period, or a new sentence. Arrows `→` are fine in diagrams (one chain per line).\n- No gendered pronouns (he, she, his, her, him, hers, himself, herself) for people in the stimulus. Use the name, \"the author\", \"the speaker\", or \"they\".\n- Never mention Logic Games. Never claim answer-position patterns. No jokes about students, no politics, no \"this is always wrong\" overbroad rules.\n- No internal labels in student text: no \"concept lesson\", \"golden set\", \"Revamp\", production metadata. No filler (\"Great question\", \"it's important to note\", \"that's the point\"). No word \"door\" as a unit of content.\n- Plain, direct, confident, short sentences. Teach the reasoning; do not assert the conclusion. Concrete beats abstract. Speak to the student as \"we\" or \"you\". A personal jab at the test is fine, sparingly.\n- Keep paragraph breaks as `\\n\\n` inside a body when helpful. Do not use markdown headers or bullets unless the existing steps already do.\n- Never change which choice is credited.\n\n## Output\nWrite JSON Lines to the output path you are given, one object per qid, in input order:\n`{\"qid\": \"...\", \"action\": \"rewrite\", \"explanation_id\": \"<from packet>\", \"steps\": [{\"row_id\": \"<uuid or null>\", \"position\": 1, \"label\": \"...\", \"body\": \"...\"}], \"notes\": [{\"row_id\": \"<uuid or null>\", \"letter\": \"A\", \"body\": \"...\"}], \"why\": \"one sentence: what was weak\", \"neighbors\": [\"qids you modelled on\"]}`\nor `{\"qid\": \"...\", \"action\": \"skip\", \"why\": \"...\"}` or `{\"qid\": \"...\", \"action\": \"flag\", \"why\": \"suspected mis-key or bank/DB mismatch: ...\"}`.\nIn a rewrite, `steps` lists ALL steps (existing in position order, then any added) and `notes` lists all five.\n\nWrite the file with a Python script that builds dicts and `json.dumps(..., ensure_ascii=False)`; put scripts only in your work directory. Double-check each item against the question before you finish.\nReply with: counts of rewrite, skip, flag; and any item you are unsure of, with the reason.\n"}

1	"""Build guarded UPDATE batches from edits-X.json + verdicts-X.jsonl.
2	Usage: python -I apply.py <edits.json> <verdicts.jsonl> <out_prefix>
3	Writes <out_prefix>-NN.sql (each <=~60KB) and <out_prefix>-final.json (the applied set, old kept as snapshot)."""
4	import json, sys, re
5	GENDER = re.compile(r"\b(he|she|him|her|his|hers|himself|herself)\b", re.I)
6	edits = json.load(open(sys.argv[1], encoding='utf-8'))
7	verd = [json.loads(l) for l in open(sys.argv[2], encoding='utf-8') if l.strip()]
8	byrow = {}
9	for v in verd: byrow[v['row_id']] = v
10	final, bad = [], []
11	for p in edits:
12	    v = byrow.get(p['row_id'])
13	    if not v: bad.append(('no verdict', p['qid'], p['row_id'])); continue
14	    if v['verdict'] == 'reject': continue
15	    new = v['new'] if v['verdict'] == 'fix' else p['new']
16	    if not new or not new.strip(): bad.append(('empty', p['qid'])); continue
17	    if new.count('—') > p['old'].count('—'): bad.append(('em dash', p['qid'], p['row_id'])); continue
18	    if len(GENDER.findall(new)) > len(GENDER.findall(p['old'])): bad.append(('pronoun', p['qid'], p['row_id'])); continue
19	    if 'logic game' in new.lower() and 'logic game' not in p['old'].lower(): bad.append(('LG', p['qid'])); continue
20	    final.append(dict(p, new=new, verdict=v['verdict']))
21	print('final', len(final), 'held back', len(bad))
22	for b in bad: print('  ', b)
23	json.dump(final, open(sys.argv[3] + '-final.json', 'w', encoding='utf-8'), ensure_ascii=False, indent=0)
24	q = lambda s: "'" + s.replace("'", "''") + "'"
25	groups = {}
26	for p in final: groups.setdefault((p['table'], p['column']), []).append(p)
27	n = 0
28	for (t, c), rows in groups.items():
29	    chunk, size = [], 0
30	    def flush():
31	        global n
32	        if not chunk: return
33	        n += 1
34	        vals = ',\n'.join(f"({q(r['row_id'])}::uuid,{q(r['old'])},{q(r['new'])})" for r in chunk)
35	        sql = (f"update {t} t set {c}=v.new from (values\n{vals}\n) v(id,old,new) "
36	               f"where t.id=v.id and t.{c}=v.old returning t.id;")
37	        open(f"{sys.argv[3]}-{n:02d}.sql", 'w', encoding='utf-8').write(sql)
38	        print(f"{sys.argv[3]}-{n:02d}.sql", t, c, len(chunk))
39	    for r in rows:
40	        s = len(r['old']) + len(r['new'])

{"file_path": "C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/699dc587-39bd-4a08-a0c2-306235d0ae46/scratchpad/step3/apply3.py", "content": "\"\"\"Build guarded SQL for step 3.\nUsage: python -I apply3.py <out_prefix> <proposals.jsonl> <verdicts.jsonl> [more pairs...]\nResolves each rewrite to its final steps/notes (proposal, or verifier's fix), runs hard gates,\nthen writes guarded UPDATEs (old body must still match) and guarded INSERTs, plus <prefix>-final.json (old kept as snapshot).\"\"\"\nimport json, sys, re, os\nsys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding='utf-8')\nHERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))\nGENDER = re.compile(r\"\\b(he|she|him|her|his|hers|himself|herself)\\b\", re.I)\nprefix = sys.argv[1]\npairs = [(sys.argv[i], sys.argv[i + 1]) for i in range(2, len(sys.argv), 2)]\nq = lambda s: \"'\" + s.replace(\"'\", \"''\") + \"'\"\nfinal, held = [], []\nfor pf, vf in pairs:\n    props = [json.loads(l) for l in open(pf, encoding='utf-8') if l.strip()]\n    verd = {}\n    for l in open(vf, encoding='utf-8'):\n        if l.strip():\n            v = json.loads(l); verd[v['qid']] = v\n    for p in props:\n        if p.get('action') != 'rewrite': continue\n        v = verd.get(p['qid'])\n        if not v: held.append((p['qid'], 'no verdict')); continue\n        if v['verdict'] not in ('approve', 'fix'): held.append((p['qid'], v['verdict'] + ': ' + v.get('note', ''))); continue\n        steps, notes = (v['new']['steps'], v['new']['notes']) if v['verdict'] == 'fix' else (p['steps'], p['notes'])\n        pk = json.load(open(f\"{HERE}/packets/{p['qid']}.json\", encoding='utf-8'))\n        old_steps = {s['row_id']: s for s in pk['steps']}\n        old_notes = {n['row_id']: n for n in pk['notes']}\n        text = ' '.join([s['body'] + ' ' + (s['label'] or '') for s in steps] + [n['body'] for n in notes])\n        old_text = ' '.join([s['body'] + ' ' + (s['label'] or '') for s in pk['steps']] + [n['body'] for n in pk['notes']])\n        err = None\n        if '—' in text or '–' in text: err = 'dash'\n        elif len(GENDER.findall(text)) > len(GENDER.findall(old_text)): err = 'pronoun'\n        elif 'logic game' in text.lower(): err = 'logic games'\n        elif sorted(n['letter'] for n in notes) != list('ABCDE'): err = 'notes not A-E'\n        elif any(not n['body'].strip() for n in notes) or any(not s['body'].strip() for s in steps): err = 'empty body'\n        elif set(old_steps) - {s['row_id'] for s in steps}: err = 'dropped a step row'\n        elif any(s['row_id'] and s['row_id'] not in old_steps for s in steps) or any(n['row_id'] and n['row_id'] not in old_notes for n in notes): err = 'unknown row id'\n        if err: held.append((p['qid'], 'gate: ' + err)); continue\n        final.append({'qid': p['qid'], 'explanation_id': pk['explanation_id'], 'steps': steps, 'notes': notes, 'old_steps': pk['steps'], 'old_notes': pk['notes']})\nprint('final', len(final), 'held', len(held))\nfor h in held: print('  HELD', h)\njson.dump(final, open(prefix + '-final.json', 'w', encoding='utf-8'), ensure_ascii=False)\nupd_steps, upd_notes, ins_steps, ins_notes = [], [], [], []\nfor f in final:\n    os_ = {s['row_id']: s for s in f['old_steps']}; on = {n['row_id']: n for n in f['old_notes']}\n    for s in f['steps']:\n        if s['row_id']:\n            o = os_[s['row_id']]\n            if o['body'] != s['body'] or (o['label'] or '') != (s['label'] or ''): upd_steps.append((s['row_id'], o['body'], s['body'], o['label'] or '', s['label'] or ''))\n        else: ins_steps.append((f['explanation_id'], s['position'], s['label'] or '', s['body']))\n    for n in f['notes']:\n        if n['row_id']:\n            o = on[n['row_id']]\n            if o['body'] != n['body']: upd_notes.append((n['row_id'], o['body'], n['body']))\n        else: ins_notes.append((f['explanation_id'], n['letter'], n['body']))\nprint('updates: steps', len(upd_steps), 'notes', len(upd_notes), '| inserts: steps', len(ins_steps), 'notes', len(ins_notes))\nfiles = []\ndef chunks(rows, size_of):\n    cur, sz = [], 0\n    for r in rows:\n        s = size_of(r)\n        if cur and sz + s > 50000: yield cur; cur, sz = [], 0\n        cur.append(r); sz += s\n    if cur: yield cur\nfor rows in chunks(upd_steps, lambda r: len(r[1]) + len(r[2]) + 200):\n    vals = ',\\n'.join(f\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(r[1])},{q(r[2])},{q(r[3])},{q(r[4])})\" for r in rows)\n    files.append(f\"update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values\\n{vals}\\n) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.ob returning t.id;\")\nfor rows in chunks(upd_notes, lambda r: len(r[1]) + len(r[2]) + 100):\n    vals = ',\\n'.join(f\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(r[1])},{q(r[2])})\" for r in rows)\n    files.append(f\"update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values\\n{vals}\\n) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.ob returning t.id;\")\nfor rows in chunks(ins_steps, lambda r: len(r[3]) + 200):\n    vals = ',\\n'.join(f\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{r[1]},{q(r[2])},{q(r[3])})\" for r in rows)\n    files.append(f\"insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\\n{vals}\\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;\")\nfor rows in chunks(ins_notes, lambda r: len(r[2]) + 150):\n    vals = ',\\n'.join(f\"({q(r[0])}::uuid,{q(r[1])},{q(r[2])})\" for r in rows)\n    files.append(f\"insert into lr_explanation_choice_notes (explanation_id,letter,body) select v.e,v.l,v.b from (values\\n{vals}\\n) v(e,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_choice_notes x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.letter=v.l) returning id;\")\nfor i, s in enumerate(files, 1):\n    open(f\"{prefix}-{i:02d}.sql\", 'w', encoding='utf-8').write(s)\nprint(len(files), 'sql files')\n"}

##### PT158-S2-Q2
[Conclusion (Clearly)] Smith's proposed ordinance does not even merit consideration.
[Evidence] Smith is now proposing an ordinance that bans the "bait-and-switch" sales tactic. But four years ago, Smith owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch.
[Evaluate] Whenever a Flaw argument rejects someone's proposal, ask what the rejection is based on. Is it the proposal itself, or the person who made it?

The council president never says a word about the ordinance. Would it work? Would it protect shoppers? Is bait-and-switch harmful? None of that comes up. The only reason given is Smith's past: Smith's store was famous for the very tactic the ordinance would ban.

That is the Ad Hominem flaw: dismissing a position because of its source rather than its content. Smith's history might make Smith look like a hypocrite, but hypocrisy doesn't make an idea bad. Given that Smith once used bait-and-switch, how could we still argue the ordinance deserves a hearing? Easily. People change. Smith may have watched customers get burned and now wants the tactic gone. Someone who knows a tactic from the inside may even be well placed to write a rule against it.

Notice what the president does not say. The president never claims the ordinance is false or mistaken, only that it "does not even merit consideration." So answers about proving a claim false miss the target. The objection is to refusing to look at the ordinance at all.
[Goal] Look for an answer describing this famous flaw; often it sounds like "the author dismisses an argument because of its source rather than its content". Otherwise, it might be a more specific answer that says, "because Person X did thing A in the past, we shouldn't listen to X's idea to do away with thing A."
A This is the prediction almost word for word. The "source" is Smith, the person who proposed the ordinance, and the president's only reason is Smith's old store. The "content" is what the ordinance would actually do, which the president never discusses. Test it: could the ordinance still be a good law even though Smith once used bait-and-switch? Yes. So the president's reason never touches the ordinance. When every reason for rejecting an idea is about the person behind it, look for this source-versus-content wording.
B A single fact that truly contradicts a claim does show the claim is false, so this often isn't even a flaw. And the president never says the ordinance is false. Smith's old store is not incompatible with the ordinance being a good law; it is a fact about the person who wrote it.
C The president never discusses sales tactics at all, deceptive or legitimate. Nothing in the argument depends on what counts as bait-and-switch, so no distinction between kinds of tactics is needed. The argument fails because it looks only at who proposed the ordinance.
D Circular reasoning would mean the premise already states the conclusion. The premise is about Smith's store four years ago; the conclusion is that the ordinance does not merit consideration. Those are different claims. The premise is about the wrong thing, but it doesn't repeat the conclusion.
E There is no generalization here. The president moves from one fact about Smith to one conclusion about Smith's ordinance and never extends the point to other councilmembers, other ordinances, or bait-and-switch in general. With no sample and no broader category, a generalization flaw has nothing to describe.
##### PT119-S2-Q24
[Conclusion] The ancient Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages.
[Evidence] An Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. depicts what appears to be a brewery, and its chemical residue shows it held alcoholic beer. That is older than the Babylonian wine-making evidence from 1500 B.C., which had been thought to be the first.
[Evaluate] "First known" and "first ever" are different claims. The cup is the earliest evidence of alcohol we have found. That puts Egypt ahead of Babylon on the evidence so far, but the conclusion says the Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages at all.

Given the 2000 B.C. cup, how could we argue the Egyptians were not first? Another society could have brewed or fermented something earlier and left no trace, or left one nobody has dug up yet. The argument even shows how this happens: the Babylonians were thought to be first until this cup turned up. The next discovery could do the same to the Egyptians.

Whenever a conclusion says something was the first, the only, or never happened, check whether the evidence covers everything or just what has been found so far.
[Goal] Find an answer that points out the gap between "earliest evidence we have" and "first ever."
A A generalization flaw would need a claim about Egyptian society as a whole, such as how common beer drinking was. The conclusion isn't about that. One cup is enough to show alcohol was being made in Egypt by 2000 B.C. The real question is whether anyone made it before then.
B The term holds steady. The premises mention Babylonian wine and Egyptian beer, and the conclusion's "alcoholic beverages" covers both in the same sense. Nothing shifts meaning between premises and conclusion. The argument compares dates, so the weak spot is about what came first, not what the words mean.
C The argument never says Babylonian wine-making depended on Egyptian brewing. It claims only that Egypt came first in time. C describes a claim about one society's technology depending on another's, and the author makes no such claim, so there is nothing for this criticism to attack.
D This is the gap we predicted. "The first known instance of a kind" is the 2000 B.C. Egyptian cup, the oldest evidence found so far. "The first instance of that kind" is what the conclusion claims: the Egyptians were the first society ever to produce alcohol. The author treats the two as the same. Test it: could some society have made alcohol before 2000 B.C. without leaving evidence we have found? Yes, and the Babylonians losing their title shows new finds can overturn an old first. When a Flaw conclusion claims a first, look for this answer.
E Arguments may state background facts without proving them, and the 1500 B.C. date mainly tells us what used to be believed. Supporting it wouldn't help the argument anyway: even a well-documented Babylonian date can't show that no society produced alcohol before 2000 B.C. The flaw is elsewhere.

2	('395281fb-7c82-4015-ac20-8df0d0b7beaa'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for the classic necessary vs. sufficient answer: the argument treats something required for greatness (a passionate response) as if it were enough for greatness. A conversational version would say the argument fails to consider that a work can evoke passionate responses without being great.'),
3	('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,1,'Conclusion','Clearly, then, these speeches are selfishly motivated and the promises made in them are unreliable.'),
4	('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,2,'Evaluate','The evidence is about motive: candidates give speeches to get elected. The conclusion has two parts. The first, that the speeches are selfishly motivated, follows closely from that motive. The second is the leap: the promises are unreliable.
5	
6	Why would a selfish motive make a promise unreliable? The author never says. This is an attack on the source rather than the content. The reason someone has for saying something doesn''t tell us whether it is true or whether it will be kept.
7	
8	Given that candidates make promises to get elected, how could we argue the promises are still reliable? A candidate who wants to win the next election also has a selfish reason to keep them, since broken promises cost votes. The motive might even make the promises more reliable. Either way, an ulterior motive alone doesn''t settle whether a promise can be trusted.'),
9	('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that a promise made for a self-interested reason can still be reliable. In formal terms: it rejects the promises because of the speaker''s motive rather than because of any evidence about the promises themselves.')
10	) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;

But four years ago, Smith owned an appliance store that was famous for its use of bait-and-switch.','Evidence','Evidence'),
3	('b8076a74-666d-40bb-83ae-1b88584557ef'::uuid,'Whenever we''re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we consider the 2 of the 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals: Ad Hominem - This refers to when an author dismisses an argument because the source of the argument has a biased interest or conflicting past behavior. Unproven vs. Proven False - This refers to when an author argues that "because there''s no evidence for X, or because someone''s attempt to prove X failed, that shows that X is false". This looks to be an example of Ad Hominem. The author isn''t saying that Smith''s claim is false, so it couldn''t be Unproven vs. Proven False. She''s just dismissing his view, saying "it doesn''t even merit consideration". And why? Because of Smith''s conflicting past behavior. We should never reject someone''s suggestion that we do Y just because that person used to say/do the opposite of Y. People and circumstances can change. Maybe there was someone who was really into cryptocurrency when it first came out but now is against it because of how bad its energy demands are for the environment. We can''t say, "Don''t listen to that crypto-critic. After all, he was way into Bitcoin when it first came out."','Whenever a Flaw argument rejects someone''s proposal, ask what the rejection is based on. Is it the proposal itself, or the person who made it?
4	
5	The council president never says a word about the ordinance. Would it work? Would it protect shoppers? Is bait-and-switch harmful? None of that comes up. The only reason given is Smith''s past: Smith''s store was famous for the very tactic the ordinance would ban.
6	
7	That is the Ad Hominem flaw: dismissing a position because of its source rather than its content. Smith''s history might make Smith look like a hypocrite, but hypocrisy doesn''t make an idea bad. Given that Smith once used bait-and-switch, how could we still argue the ordinance deserves a hearing? Easily. People change. Smith may have watched customers get burned and now wants the tactic gone. Someone who knows a tactic from the inside may even be well placed to write a rule against it.
8	
9	Notice what the president does not say. The president never claims the ordinance is false or mistaken, only that it "does not even merit consideration." So answers about proving a claim false miss the target. The objection is to refusing to look at the ordinance at all.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
10	('bb7e7ca7-6bf8-4c7c-b7da-9337f46059bf'::uuid,'Great works of art evoke passionate responses in those who view them. 15	('5ccb5709-390f-4679-a7b8-88c20b32ce20'::uuid,'His art is great. (GW)','Reilly''s art is great.

19	The evidence tells us Reilly''s work has the passionate response. Then the conclusion jumps to greatness. That runs the arrow backward:
20	
21	Premise: GW → PR
22	Author''s move: PR, so GW
23	
24	In order to draw this conclusion, we must assume that any work of art which elicits a passionate response is a great work. That is an illegal reversal of the first premise. The rule tells us what follows from being great. It says nothing about what follows from stirring up strong feelings.
25	
26	Think of a painting viewers find offensive, or a clumsy portrait that makes people furious. Both provoke intense reactions without being great art. A passionate response is a requirement for greatness, not proof of it.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
27	('56f41c0f-785b-49e6-9cc6-3a251b6c033c'::uuid,'The companies only list voice mail''s advantages. 28	
29	Given that voice mail beats answering machines on all three, how could we argue answering machines will still survive? The list only covers what voice mail is good at. It never asks whether answering machines do something voice mail can''t. If they offer one valuable feature voice mail lacks, some customers will keep their machines for that feature alone, and the machines won''t become obsolete.
30	
31	Notice how strong the conclusion is: "obsolete." To weaken it, we don''t have to show answering machines will win. We only need a reason a meaningful number of people would keep using them.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
32	('773dcb3b-ca9e-4a32-a0e4-b56b8ebab524'::uuid,'Find an advantage answering machines have over voice mail.','Look for an advantage answering machines have that voice mail does not offer, something useful enough that people would keep their machines for it. 33	('718bd96e-cea7-49a0-b1b6-9a55ffe0fb3b'::uuid,'Pause and infer. Technology brings more food, but the centralization it needs makes collapse deadlier. So technology doesn''t remove the dangers of collapse. If anything, the route to more food makes them worse.','Pause and infer before reading the choices. Line up the two relationships:
34	
35	Better technology → more food, but only a negligible increase without centralization
36	More centralization → a greater share of people perish if the society collapses
37	
38	So the technology route to more food runs through centralization, and centralization makes collapse deadlier. That is why the author concludes the effort will "merely exacerbate the disasters associated with societal collapse."
39	
40	What does that tell us about technology and collapse? If better technology could prevent every problem that comes with a collapse, the author couldn''t say the technology-driven plan only makes those disasters worse. So at least one collapse problem, the higher death toll, is something technology does not fix.
41	
42	On Most Strongly Supported questions, a weakly worded answer like "not every" is easy to prove. It needs only one exception, and the stimulus hands us one.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
43	('7c2f4e9a-fca0-4474-a21b-87372a6535a2'::uuid,'Technology won''t prevent every problem that comes with a centralized society''s collapse.','Technology won''t prevent every problem that comes with a centralized society''s collapse. 44	('e29f83ec-ca14-4f4a-92d1-f1a4fdfcbbc6'::uuid,'An Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. depicts a brewery and contains residue of alcoholic beer — older than the Babylonian fermentation evidence from 1500 B.C.','An Egyptian cup dating from 2000 B.C. depicts what appears to be a brewery, and its chemical residue shows it held alcoholic beer. That is older than the Babylonian wine-making evidence from 1500 B.C., which had been thought to be the first.','Evidence','Evidence'),
45	('df1b22a1-32f7-4e82-bcfd-fd61bec76946'::uuid,'"First known" and "first ever" are different. The Egyptian cup is the earliest *archaeological evidence* of alcoholic beverage production we have found, but other societies — including the Egyptians themselves earlier or some entirely different civilization — could have produced alcohol earlier without leaving evidence behind, or with evidence we have not yet found.','"First known" and "first ever" are different claims. The cup is the earliest evidence of alcohol we have found. That puts Egypt ahead of Babylon on the evidence so far, but the conclusion says the Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages at all.
46	
47	Given the 2000 B.C. cup, how could we argue the Egyptians were not first? Another society could have brewed or fermented something earlier and left no trace, or left one nobody has dug up yet. The argument even shows how this happens: the Babylonians were thought to be first until this cup turned up. The next discovery could do the same to the Egyptians.
48	
49	Whenever a conclusion says something was the first, the only, or never happened, check whether the evidence covers everything or just what has been found so far.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
50	('688ff509-a7ab-4c06-b061-692fbc4d62ad'::uuid,'Political candidates’ speeches are loaded with promises and with expressions of good intention.

56	
57	But one must not forget that the politicians'' purpose in giving these speeches is to get themselves elected.','Evidence','Evidence')

The Egyptian cup is the earliest *archaeological evidence* of alcoholic beverage production we have found, but other societies — including the Egyptians themselves earlier or some entirely different civilization — could have produced alcohol earlier without leaving evidence behind, or with evidence we have not yet found.','\"First known\" and \"first ever\" are different claims. The cup is the earliest evidence of alcohol we have found. That puts Egypt ahead of Babylon on the evidence so far, but the conclusion says the Egyptians were the first society to produce alcoholic beverages at all.\n\nGiven the 2000 B.C. cup, how could we argue the Egyptians were not first? Another society could have brewed or fermented something earlier and left no trace, or left one nobody has dug up yet. The argument even shows how this happens: the Babylonians were thought to be first until this cup turned up. The next discovery could do the same to the Egyptians.\n\nWhenever a conclusion says something was the first, the only, or never happened, check whether the evidence covers everything or just what has been found so far.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('688ff509-a7ab-4c06-b061-692fbc4d62ad'::uuid,'Political candidates’ speeches are loaded with promises and with expressions of good intention.\nBut one must not forget that the politicians’ purpose in giving these speeches is to get themselves elected.\nConclusion\nClearly, then, these speeches are selfishly motivated and the promises made in them are unreliable.\nEvaluate\nThis is an ad hominem attack. Just because politicians are motivated to win election does not mean that their promises are unreliable.','Political candidates'' speeches are loaded with promises and with expressions of good intention.\n\nBut one must not forget that the politicians'' purpose in giving these speeches is to get themselves elected.','Evidence','Evidence')\n) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.ob returning t.id;"}

1	update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values
2	('fe20e582-d97b-4f70-8f45-7289d1888aea'::uuid,'This answer precisely identifies the argument''s flaw. The council president dismisses the proposed ordinance entirely because of who proposed it — Smith, a former bait-and-switch practitioner — rather than because of any problem with the ordinance itself. The merits of a proposal are logically independent of the personal history of its author. Smith''s past use of bait-and-switch might make him a hypocrite, but it says nothing about whether the ordinance is well-crafted, enforceable, or beneficial to consumers. By attacking the source rather than the content, the president avoids engaging with the substance of the proposal entirely. This is a classic ad hominem fallacy: the argument treats biographical information about the proposer as grounds for rejecting the proposition.','This is the prediction almost word for word. The "source" is Smith, the person who proposed the ordinance, and the president''s only reason is Smith''s old store. The "content" is what the ordinance would actually do, which the president never discusses. Test it: could the ordinance still be a good law even though Smith once used bait-and-switch? Yes. So the president''s reason never touches the ordinance. When every reason for rejecting an idea is about the person behind it, look for this source-versus-content wording.'),
3	('21daac46-1951-4f64-b320-b945b4ac83dc'::uuid,'This answer describes using a single incompatible fact to dismiss a claim as false. But the argument does not work that way. The president is not identifying a fact that contradicts the ordinance and using it to prove the ordinance is wrong. Instead, the president is using a fact about Smith''s personal history to dismiss the ordinance without evaluating it. The distinction matters: the incompatibility described in this answer would be between evidence and the claim being evaluated, but here the "evidence" (Smith''s history) has no logical bearing on the claim (the ordinance''s merit).
4	
5	Even if an argument did use a single incompatible fact to reject a claim, that is not inherently fallacious. A single counterexample can legitimately refute a universal claim. The flaw described here — relying on one piece of contradictory evidence — is a valid form of reasoning in many contexts, not a logical error. The actual flaw in the argument is ad hominem reasoning, not insufficient evidence against the proposal.','A single fact that truly contradicts a claim does show the claim is false, so this often isn''t even a flaw. And the president never says the ordinance is false. Smith''s old store is not incompatible with the ordinance being a good law; it is a fact about the person who wrote it.'),
6	('c37e9689-cc6e-4dc1-95a0-005311fe4061'::uuid,'This answer suggests the argument fails to distinguish between deceptive and legitimate sales tactics. While such a distinction could theoretically be relevant to a discussion about regulating sales practices, it does not describe the actual flaw in this argument. The president never discusses the substance of sales tactics at all — the president never addresses what the ordinance would prohibit or whether bait-and-switch is appropriately classified. The entire argument operates at the level of "who proposed it" rather than "what it proposes." The fundamental flaw is the ad hominem attack on Smith, not a failure to make nuanced distinctions about different types of sales tactics.','The president never discusses sales tactics at all, deceptive or legitimate. Nothing in the argument depends on what counts as bait-and-switch, so no distinction between kinds of tactics is needed. The argument fails because it looks only at who proposed the ordinance.'),
7	('1aaa836f-8659-4cb1-8e78-54f6fba46fe0'::uuid,'Circular reasoning occurs when the conclusion simply restates one of its own premises — when the argument assumes what it is trying to prove. For this to apply, the president''s conclusion (the ordinance does not merit consideration) would need to be the same as one of the premises. But the premise is that Smith used bait-and-switch four years ago, and the conclusion is that his ordinance should be dismissed. These are different claims. The argument may be invalid (because the premise is irrelevant to the conclusion), but it is not circular — it does not assume its own conclusion. Circular arguments at least have the right kind of connection between premise and conclusion; this argument''s problem is the wrong kind of connection entirely.','Circular reasoning would mean the premise already states the conclusion. The premise is about Smith''s store four years ago; the conclusion is that the ordinance does not merit consideration. Those are different claims. The premise is about the wrong thing, but it doesn''t repeat the conclusion.'),
8	('04933fd5-a80b-4a9e-9426-74683bb5986e'::uuid,'A sampling or generalization flaw occurs when a conclusion about a broader category is drawn from an unrepresentative or insufficient sample. The president is not generalizing from instances at all. The argument does not say "Smith''s store used bait-and-switch and was bad, therefore all bait-and-switch is bad" or "one case proves a pattern." The president''s reasoning is entirely about Smith as an individual and his relationship to the ordinance he proposed. The flaw is about the type of evidence used (biographical rather than substantive), not about the quantity of instances examined. No sampling, surveying, or generalizing is involved in the argument.','There is no generalization here. The president moves from one fact about Smith to one conclusion about Smith''s ordinance and never extends the point to other councilmembers, other ordinances, or bait-and-switch in general. With no sample and no broader category, a generalization flaw has nothing to describe.'),
9	('ed3f23fc-7335-48a5-ab29-cb6c56562dab'::uuid,'Circular reasoning occurs when a premise is essentially a restatement of the conclusion — the argument assumes what it is trying to prove. That is not happening here. The premise that great art evokes passionate responses is not the same as the conclusion that Reilly''s art is great. The premise about Reilly''s work evoking emotional responses is also not a restatement of the conclusion. These are distinct claims. The argument''s problem is that it reverses the conditional, not that it assumes its own conclusion.','Circular reasoning would mean a premise already assumes Reilly''s art is great. Neither premise does. One is a general rule about great art; the other reports how viewers react to Reilly''s work. The conclusion adds something new. The trouble is the direction the rule is applied in, not a premise that assumes the answer.'),
10	('9f62d0be-f013-44f2-9602-36d8d2ad58ff'::uuid,'This precisely describes the argument''s flaw. The first premise establishes that evoking passionate responses is a necessary condition for being great art — all great art has this quality. The argument then treats this necessary condition as if it were also sufficient — as if having this quality guarantees greatness. But many things that are not great art could evoke passionate responses. Provocative, disturbing, or even terrible art can elicit intense emotions. The argument assumes that because Reilly''s art satisfies a necessary condition for greatness, it must therefore be great. This is the formal fallacy of affirming the consequent, equivalently described as confusing necessary and sufficient conditions.','This is the prediction in formal language. "A condition that is necessary for having a certain quality" is evoking passionate responses, which every great work must do. "As if it must also be sufficient" is the author''s move: Reilly''s work has the passionate response, so it must be great. Check it by plugging in the terms: did the author treat a passionate response as enough for greatness? Yes, that is the whole argument. Whenever a Flaw argument gives you "if X, then Y," shows Y, and concludes X, look for this answer.'),
11	('c264dadb-e5fd-40a0-9fc4-26d2b5811dda'::uuid,'This answer suggests the argument misapplies a general claim to an atypical instance. But there is no reason to believe Reilly''s work is atypical of the class being discussed. The argument applies the claim "great art evokes passion" to Reilly''s work, which does evoke passion. The problem is not that Reilly''s work is an unusual case that does not fit the general claim — it is that the argument reverses the direction of the conditional. Even if Reilly''s work were perfectly typical, the argument would still be flawed.','For C to fit, Reilly''s work would need to be an unusual case that the general claim doesn''t really cover. Nothing suggests that. The rule about great art isn''t misapplied to an odd case; it is run backward. Even if Reilly were a perfectly typical painter, the reversal would still be a flaw.'),
12	('d107ecbb-6eba-4813-a22c-25320516fdc7'::uuid,'This answer describes a hasty generalization — drawing a broad conclusion from too few instances. The argument is not making a generalization at all. It is making a specific claim about one artist''s work (Reilly''s art is great). There is no indication that the argument uses an insufficient number of Reilly''s works or an insufficient number of viewer reactions. The flaw is structural — reversing a conditional — not statistical.','A hasty generalization builds a broad claim from a small sample. The argument''s general claim, that great works evoke passionate responses, is offered as a premise, not drawn from examples. The conclusion is about one painter''s work. No sample, no generalization to criticize.'),
13	('63816d8a-5592-4f98-9776-424061a296a8'::uuid,'This answer claims the argument draws a value judgment from non-value premises. But the first premise is itself a value statement: "great works of art evoke passionate responses" contains the value-laden term "great." The conclusion ("his art is great") and the premise both involve the concept of artistic quality. So the argument does not lack value-laden premises — it has one. The flaw is not about moving from factual premises to value conclusions; it is about reversing the direction of a conditional relationship within those value claims.','Check the premises for value language. The first premise is about great works of art, and "great" is the same value judgment the conclusion makes about Reilly. So the argument does start from a premise involving values. E describes a gap this argument does not have.'),
14	('3f57b890-d9c3-440f-8bac-4b262ff5ad2d'::uuid,'Points out an advantage answering machines have over voicemail.','This is the missing advantage we predicted. With an answering machine, you can hear who is calling and then decide whether to pick up. Voice mail can''t do that. Anyone who likes screening calls now has a reason to keep a machine even if voice mail is cheaper and more convenient, so the machines may not become obsolete. When an argument predicts that a new option will replace an old one, look for a benefit the old option keeps that the new one lacks.'),
15	('b465e72d-0902-4d8a-8703-0ed208525611'::uuid,'This is the most popular wrong answer. The passage ties centralization to deaths *in a collapse*, not to a greater need for food. Food production is what drives centralization, not the other way around.','The passage links centralization to one thing: how many people perish when a society collapses. It never says centralized societies need more food. The link it does give runs the other way: the push for more food is the reason to centralize. A reverses that, making centralization the cause of a greater need for food.'),
16	('9ad6805f-2308-4a22-a1e5-2f6a4117aead'::uuid,'Centralizing for more food makes collapses worse, so technology plainly doesn''t fix every collapse problem. "Not every" asks for very little.','B asks for very little. "Not every" problem means at least one problem survives the technology. The passage supplies one: the technology route to more food requires centralization, and centralization makes collapse deadlier. Technology doesn''t prevent that higher death toll; the author says the effort makes it worse. When an answer is this modestly worded, check whether the stimulus gives you one clear case. Here it does.'),
17	('95d2ac70-01e5-4301-bab1-276df99e9c34'::uuid,'Population growth continuing "indefinitely" is far beyond anything here.','The passage says populations will increase, but nothing about the rate of growth, let alone a rate that keeps rising forever. "Continue to increase indefinitely" is a claim about the future that no statement here supports.'),
18	('dce8601f-8057-4639-996a-c9a8db1a766b'::uuid,'"Only by technology" is never claimed.','The passage says technology will enable food production to increase. It never says technology is the only way to do it. If anything, it stresses centralization as a requirement for meaningful increases. To support "only by improved technology," we would need to rule out every other route, and nothing does.'),
19	('d2087976-f065-4a03-9d00-53af8db03539'::uuid,'The passage gives no history of centralization rising with technology. It talks about what *would* happen.','The passage''s history covers one relationship: more centralized societies lost a greater share of their people when they collapsed. It never says societies became more centralized as technology improved. The centralization it discusses is something societies would have to undertake, not a trend it reports.'),
20	('88fa80c1-6a8d-4f41-a4e2-06bc9b93e03e'::uuid,'The author is not using one Egyptian cup to generalize about what most Egyptians did. The cup establishes an instance of alcoholic beer in Egypt by 2000 B.C.; the issue is whether that instance was the world''s first.','A generalization flaw would need a claim about Egyptian society as a whole, such as how common beer drinking was. The conclusion isn''t about that. One cup is enough to show alcohol was being made in Egypt by 2000 B.C. The real question is whether anyone made it before then.'),
21	('f0375ec8-e3e1-4fef-ba3d-2bfba1f62a19'::uuid,'"Alcoholic beverage" includes wine and beer in both the evidence and the conclusion. The argument compares dates, not two meanings of the term.','The term holds steady. The premises mention Babylonian wine and Egyptian beer, and the conclusion''s "alcoholic beverages" covers both in the same sense. Nothing shifts meaning between premises and conclusion. The argument compares dates, so the weak spot is about what came first, not what the words mean.'),
22	('3de5d70d-4fdc-4b17-9043-13b657a42b57'::uuid,'The author does not say Babylonian wine production depended on Egyptian beer production. Being earlier than one known Babylonian example is offered as evidence for priority, not for transmission of technology.','The argument never says Babylonian wine-making depended on Egyptian brewing. It claims only that Egypt came first in time. C describes a claim about one society''s technology depending on another''s, and the author makes no such claim, so there is nothing for this criticism to attack.'),
23	('4a8201c9-e781-4de8-94ba-6bcce841130c'::uuid,'The cup is older than the cited Babylonian evidence, so Egypt has the earlier *known* example of the two. Another society might have made alcohol even earlier without leaving evidence we have found. The oldest discovered instance need not be the first historical instance.','This is the gap we predicted. "The first known instance of a kind" is the 2000 B.C. Egyptian cup, the oldest evidence found so far. "The first instance of that kind" is what the conclusion claims: the Egyptians were the first society ever to produce alcohol. The author treats the two as the same. Test it: could some society have made alcohol before 2000 B.C. without leaving evidence we have found? Yes, and the Babylonians losing their title shows new finds can overturn an old first. When a Flaw conclusion claims a first, look for this answer.'),
24	('81ca6f1f-1b28-4e5e-a272-c34f0e0e98a1'::uuid,'The author supplies the 1500 B.C. Babylonian wine date as background. Even if that date were well documented, the Egyptian cup would not establish that no society produced alcohol before Egypt.','Arguments may state background facts without proving them, and the 1500 B.C. date mainly tells us what used to be believed. Supporting it wouldn''t help the argument anyway: even a well-documented Babylonian date can''t show that no society produced alcohol before 2000 B.C. The flaw is elsewhere.'),
25	('a8162afd-26ef-4db8-ae4d-e24315bdde22'::uuid,'This negates the assumption of the argument that if a person''s promise is selfishly motivated then that promise is unreliable.','The argument assumes that a selfishly motivated promise is unreliable. A negates both halves of that rule: a promise that is not selfishly motivated is reliable. That is a different conditional, and the argument never needs it. It says nothing about unselfish promises at all, so it isn''t presuming anything about them.'),
26	('9621931d-ac06-4316-ba83-092ba343f2e5'::uuid,'The argument does not go so far as to say that such promises are never kept, but rather says that they are unreliable.','"Never kept" is far stronger than "unreliable." An unreliable promise is one we can''t count on; it might still be kept some of the time. The argument only needs selfish promises to be untrustworthy, not broken every time, so it doesn''t presume what B describes.'),
27	('82f4a5c5-d965-4586-9952-b263dfe274c8'::uuid,'This describes a causal reasoning error: confusing an effect with its cause. That flaw isn''t in this argument, which moves from a selfish motive to the claim that the promises are unreliable.','Confusing cause and effect would mean treating a result as if it were the reason. The argument doesn''t do that. It names the candidates'' purpose, getting elected, as the motive behind the speeches, which is exactly what a purpose is. The flaw is the jump from that motive to unreliable promises.'),
28	('22db832a-30a1-4d0c-8c23-319b3eb9a985'::uuid,'This correctly describes the difference between a questionable source and an unreliable claim.','This matches the prediction. The "ulterior motive" is the candidates'' purpose of getting elected, and "a promise need not be unreliable" denies the second half of the conclusion. The argument moves from why a promise was made to whether it can be trusted, and D points out that the first doesn''t settle the second. Whenever an argument rejects a claim because of the speaker''s motives, look for an answer that separates the motive from the merit of what was said.')
29	) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.ob returning t.id;

But the first premise is itself a value statement: \"great works of art evoke passionate responses\" contains the value-laden term \"great.\" The conclusion (\"his art is great\") and the premise both involve the concept of artistic quality. So the argument does not lack value-laden premises — it has one. The flaw is not about moving from factual premises to value conclusions; it is about reversing the direction of a conditional relationship within those value claims.','Check the premises for value language. The first premise is about great works of art, and \"great\" is the same value judgment the conclusion makes about Reilly. So the argument does start from a premise involving values. E describes a gap this argument does not have.'),\n('3f57b890-d9c3-440f-8bac-4b262ff5ad2d'::uuid,'Points out an advantage answering machines have over voicemail.','This is the missing advantage we predicted. With an answering machine, you can hear who is calling and then decide whether to pick up. Voice mail can''t do that. Anyone who likes screening calls now has a reason to keep a machine even if voice mail is cheaper and more convenient, so the machines may not become obsolete. When an argument predicts that a new option will replace an old one, look for a benefit the old option keeps that the new one lacks.'),\n('b465e72d-0902-4d8a-8703-0ed208525611'::uuid,'This is the most popular wrong answer. The passage ties centralization to deaths *in a collapse*, not to a greater need for food. Food production is what drives centralization, not the other way around.','The passage links centralization to one thing: how many people perish when a society collapses. It never says centralized societies need more food. The link it does give runs the other way: the push for more food is the reason to centralize. A reverses that, making centralization the cause of a greater need for food.'),\n('9ad6805f-2308-4a22-a1e5-2f6a4117aead'::uuid,'Centralizing for more food makes collapses worse, so technology plainly doesn''t fix every collapse problem. \"Not every\" asks for very little.','B asks for very little. \"Not every\" problem means at least one problem survives the technology. The passage supplies one: the technology route to more food requires centralization, and centralization makes collapse deadlier. Technology doesn''t prevent that higher death toll; the author says the effort makes it worse. When an answer is this modestly worded, check whether the stimulus gives you one clear case. Here it does.'),\n('95d2ac70-01e5-4301-bab1-276df99e9c34'::uuid,'Population growth continuing \"indefinitely\" is far beyond anything here.','The passage says populations will increase, but nothing about the rate of growth, let alone a rate that keeps rising forever. \"Continue to increase indefinitely\" is a claim about the future that no statement here supports.'),\n('dce8601f-8057-4639-996a-c9a8db1a766b'::uuid,'\"Only by technology\" is never claimed.','The passage says technology will enable food production to increase. It never says technology is the only way to do it. If anything, it stresses centralization as a requirement for meaningful increases. To support \"only by improved technology,\" we would need to rule out every other route, and nothing does.'),\n('d2087976-f065-4a03-9d00-53af8db03539'::uuid,'The passage gives no history of centralization rising with technology. It talks about what *would* happen.','The passage''s history covers one relationship: more centralized societies lost a greater share of their people when they collapsed. It never says societies became more centralized as technology improved. The centralization it discusses is something societies would have to undertake, not a trend it reports.'),\n('88fa80c1-6a8d-4f41-a4e2-06bc9b93e03e'::uuid,'The author is not using one Egyptian cup to generalize about what most Egyptians did. The cup establishes an instance of alcoholic beer in Egypt by 2000 B.C.; the issue is whether that instance was the world''s first.','A generalization flaw would need a claim about Egyptian society as a whole, such as how common beer drinking was. The conclusion isn''t about that. One cup is enough to show alcohol was being made in Egypt by 2000 B.C. The real question is whether anyone made it before then.'),\n('f0375ec8-e3e1-4fef-ba3d-2bfba1f62a19'::uuid,'\"Alcoholic beverage\" includes wine and beer in both the evidence and the conclusion. The argument compares dates, not two meanings of the term.','The term holds steady. The premises mention Babylonian wine and Egyptian beer, and the conclusion''s \"alcoholic beverages\" covers both in the same sense. Nothing shifts meaning between premises and conclusion. The argument compares dates, so the weak spot is about what came first, not what the words mean.'),\n('3de5d70d-4fdc-4b17-9043-13b657a42b57'::uuid,'The author does not say Babylonian wine production depended on Egyptian beer production. Being earlier than one known Babylonian example is offered as evidence for priority, not for transmission of technology.','The argument never says Babylonian wine-making depended on Egyptian brewing. It claims only that Egypt came first in time. C describes a claim about one society''s technology depending on another''s, and the author makes no such claim, so there is nothing for this criticism to attack.'),\n('4a8201c9-e781-4de8-94ba-6bcce841130c'::uuid,'The cup is older than the cited Babylonian evidence, so Egypt has the earlier *known* example of the two. Another society might have made alcohol even earlier without leaving evidence we have found. The oldest discovered instance need not be the first historical instance.','This is the gap we predicted. \"The first known instance of a kind\" is the 2000 B.C. Egyptian cup, the oldest evidence found so far. \"The first instance of that kind\" is what the conclusion claims: the Egyptians were the first society ever to produce alcohol. The author treats the two as the same. Test it: could some society have made alcohol before 2000 B.C. without leaving evidence we have found? Yes, and the Babylonians losing their title shows new finds can overturn an old first. When a Flaw conclusion claims a first, look for this answer.'),\n('81ca6f1f-1b28-4e5e-a272-c34f0e0e98a1'::uuid,'The author supplies the 1500 B.C. Babylonian wine date as background. Even if that date were well documented, the Egyptian cup would not establish that no society produced alcohol before Egypt.','Arguments may state background facts without proving them, and the 1500 B.C. date mainly tells us what used to be believed. Supporting it wouldn''t help the argument anyway: even a well-documented Babylonian date can''t show that no society produced alcohol before 2000 B.C. The flaw is elsewhere.'),\n('a8162afd-26ef-4db8-ae4d-e24315bdde22'::uuid,'This negates the assumption of the argument that if a person''s promise is selfishly motivated then that promise is unreliable.','The argument assumes that a selfishly motivated promise is unreliable. A negates both halves of that rule: a promise that is not selfishly motivated is reliable. That is a different conditional, and the argument never needs it. It says nothing about unselfish promises at all, so it isn''t presuming anything about them.'),\n('9621931d-ac06-4316-ba83-092ba343f2e5'::uuid,'The argument does not go so far as to say that such promises are never kept, but rather says that they are unreliable.','\"Never kept\" is far stronger than \"unreliable.\" An unreliable promise is one we can''t count on; it might still be kept some of the time. The argument only needs selfish promises to be untrustworthy, not broken every time, so it doesn''t presume what B describes.'),\n('82f4a5c5-d965-4586-9952-b263dfe274c8'::uuid,'This describes a causal reasoning error: confusing an effect with its cause. That flaw isn''t in this argument, which moves from a selfish motive to the claim that the promises are unreliable.','Confusing cause and effect would mean treating a result as if it were the reason. The argument doesn''t do that. It names the candidates'' purpose, getting elected, as the motive behind the speeches, which is exactly what a purpose is. The flaw is the jump from that motive to unreliable promises.'),\n('22db832a-30a1-4d0c-8c23-319b3eb9a985'::uuid,'This correctly describes the difference between a questionable source and an unreliable claim.','This matches the prediction. The \"ulterior motive\" is the candidates'' purpose of getting elected, and \"a promise need not be unreliable\" denies the second half of the conclusion. The argument moves from why a promise was made to whether it can be trusted, and D points out that the first doesn''t settle the second. Whenever an argument rejects a claim because of the speaker''s motives, look for an answer that separates the motive from the merit of what was said.')\n) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and t.body=v.ob returning t.id;"}

{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\n('395281fb-7c82-4015-ac20-8df0d0b7beaa'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for the classic necessary vs. sufficient answer: the argument treats something required for greatness (a passionate response) as if it were enough for greatness. A conversational version would say the argument fails to consider that a work can evoke passionate responses without being great.'),\n('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,1,'Conclusion','Clearly, then, these speeches are selfishly motivated and the promises made in them are unreliable.'),\n('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,2,'Evaluate','The evidence is about motive: candidates give speeches to get elected. The conclusion has two parts. The first, that the speeches are selfishly motivated, follows closely from that motive. The second is the leap: the promises are unreliable.\n\nWhy would a selfish motive make a promise unreliable? The author never says. This is an attack on the source rather than the content. The reason someone has for saying something doesn''t tell us whether it is true or whether it will be kept.\n\nGiven that candidates make promises to get elected, how could we argue the promises are still reliable? A candidate who wants to win the next election also has a selfish reason to keep them, since broken promises cost votes. The motive might even make the promises more reliable. Either way, an ulterior motive alone doesn''t settle whether a promise can be trusted.'),\n('785f2e9b-575c-46db-baf6-f3c2b5be2798'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that a promise made for a self-interested reason can still be reliable. In formal terms: it rejects the promises because of the speaker''s motive rather than because of any evidence about the promises themselves.')\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;"}
