<result>All 8 items are rewrites: 0 skips, 0 flags. I solved each question myself first and agreed with the credited answer every time, so nothing is flagged as mis-keyed. The proposals are in `out-03.jsonl`. Nothing was written to any database.

The file passes the brief's mechanical checks:
- No em or en dashes, and no gendered pronouns.
- Every old step's `row_id` is kept and in its original position.
- Each item has exactly five notes, A to E, each reusing its letter's `row_id`.

Three items (PT157-S3-Q14, PT157-S3-Q17, PT157-S3-Q9) had no prediction step, so each gained a new Goal step with a null `row_id`.

Every one of the eight was in the old PT157/158 register, with em dashes and padded, untested wrong-answer notes. Beyond that, these items had real problems in the old text:
- **PT157-S2-Q9:** the old Evaluate and Goal were aimed at the wrong flaw (side effects on overall learning). The rewrite is built around the actual gap: no evidence that month-long breaks avoid the same forgetting.
- **PT158-S2-Q18:** the old Evaluate and Goal chased the word "necessary" instead of the real gap. The rewrite uses a worked example: one practitioner gets 50% on sprains and 12% on fractures, the other the reverse, so both average 31% while the choice matters a lot for any one patient. It also fixes a typo in the Evidence step ("or a by a specialist"). The packet's known problem (the A note "argues against a claim A does not make") is stale: that note is accurate, and I kept its logic. The real fault was the E note, which brought in an outside "specialists take harder cases" story; the rewrite fixes that.
- **PT158-S2-Q14:** the old Evaluate mocked the argument and used "she" throughout. Both are gone.
- **PT157-S3-Q9:** the first-person wine joke is replaced with a "drive traffic to a web page" analogy.
- **PT158-S2-Q10:** the D note on the "third civilization" trap now runs the test on it. Even if a third civilization invented the style, the conclusion still holds, so the argument never needed to rule one out.

Two items the verifier should look at closely:
1. **PT158-S2-Q18, choice C:** I eliminate it because the evidence is records of whether patients improved, not patients' opinions of their practitioners. That reading of "Records reveal" is sound, but someone could argue the records might be patient-reported.
2. **PT157-S2-Q9, intermediate conclusion:** I kept the old label "Intermediate Conclusion" on the "will insure" claim, though the word "since" could also make it a premise. Only the Evaluate and Goal bodies changed in that item.

My quote check flagged a few quoted phrases that don't appear in the question. Each one is one of my own paraphrases ("fails to consider", "more acts are now called crimes", and the like), not a misquote of the stimulus or choices. Every phrase quoted from the question matches the bank wording.

- out-03.jsonl

<result>I wrote proposals for all 8 qids to `S3/out-06.jsonl`: **8 rewrite, 0 skip, 0 flag**. I agree with the key on every one. Nothing was written to any database.

| qid | Type | Key | Main fixes |
|---|---|---|---|
| PT158-S2-Q13 | Main Conclusion | B | Removed the "She" in Evaluate; filled out the one-line Goal; replaced the em-dash notes with support-test notes, longest on D (last-claim trap) |
| PT158-S3-Q10 | Main Conclusion | B | Evidence had dropped "most" from the decay premise; Evaluate rewritten with a clean diagram; Goal now quotes the actual sentence |
| PT158-S3-Q3 | Main Conclusion | D | Goal used "she" for the columnist; B note now explains the swap of "not required" for "not enough" with a test |
| PT158-S4-Q10 | Main Conclusion | A | The old B note called "automation means fewer workers" a supported intermediate conclusion, which the stimulus never supports; also removed the overbroad claim that Main Conclusion questions never use "therefore"-type words |
| PT158-S4-Q8 | Main Conclusion | C | Evaluate said "first case" for "first sentence" and never ran the support test; the repeated "building blocks / edifice" notes are replaced with ones tied to each choice's wording, with B (overstates "not justified" as "false") as the trap |
| PT117-S3-Q8 | Method | A | The notes called Rossi "he" throughout; Evaluate and Goal were one line each |
| PT119-S4-Q2 | Method | B | A and E notes were bare verdicts; added the "argues X by Y" test (X must match the conclusion, Y the evidence) |
| PT158-S3-Q22 | Method | C | The Conclusion step said "does not provide any grounds," stronger than the stimulus's "insufficient reason"; Goal had a typo ("ti") |

Every step kept its `row_id` and its 0-based position, with no steps added. Steps that were already sound stay word for word. Each item has exactly five notes, each reusing its row. My build script checked every step and note: no em or en dashes, and no gendered pronouns.

Three things to know:
- **The `known_problem` notes for PT117-S3-Q8 and PT119-S4-Q2 are stale.** They say all five choice notes are missing, but both now have notes. I rewrote them anyway because of the pronoun problem and the bare verdicts.
- **I dropped two "most popular wrong answer" claims** (PT117 D, PT119 C). I had no data to confirm them. The longest trap notes elsewhere are my own judgment of which answer tempts most, not real answer-pick numbers.
- **PT158-S3-Q10 Evaluate keeps the general "the last sentence is rarely the answer" rule**, and PT158-S4-Q8 keeps its "90% of the time" heuristic from the house text. They're stated as rules of thumb, not data, but a strict verifier may want them checked.

The script is `S3/work-06/build.py`.</result>

2	('9bfcc23e-1bbd-44ba-962f-dadd6f3f30d1'::uuid,'causal (by, which has enabled, because, increased)
3	electrical >> play w/ >> avg # of musicians instruments more volume per band decreased
4	pivot (nevertheless)
5	electrical >> greater # of professional instruments musicians','Causal chain:
6	Electrified instruments → each musician plays louder → fewer musicians per band, on average
7	
8	Pivot (nevertheless):
9	Electrified instruments → more musicians playing popular music professionally','Facts','Facts'),
10	('981bba23-7b58-4924-a7f4-1cb69e2341a9'::uuid,'The #1 thing we read for on Most Supported is Cause / Effect relationships, and we got four of them here.
11	There''s a causal chain, and then a pivot that creates a **mathy tension** between two different things caused by electrification: 1. fewer musicians per band 2. more musicians overall When we see a Pivot creating a mathy tension, the correct answer usually addresses / resolves that tension.
12	If we have fewer musicians in each band nowadays, how can also have more musicians total?
13	We would need there to be more bands than before.
14	Maybe there used to be 10 ppl / band, and there were 80 bands, giving us 800 musicians.
15	If we only have 5 ppl / band now and want to end up with 1000 musicians (some larger number), then we''d need 200 bands.','On a Most Supported question, cause and effect is the first thing to read for, and this paragraph is full of it: electrification enabled more volume, the extra volume shrank the average band, and electrification also raised the number of professional musicians.
16	
17	Then the pivot word "Nevertheless" sets two results of electrification against each other:
18	1. Fewer musicians per band, on average.
19	2. More professional musicians overall.
20	
21	When a pivot creates a math tension like this, the correct answer usually resolves it. So ask: if each band has fewer people, how can there be more people in total?
22	
23	There have to be more bands. Run numbers. Say there used to be 80 bands of 10, which is 800 musicians. If bands now average 5 and we have 1,000 musicians, we need 200 bands. Smaller bands plus more musicians forces the number of bands up.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
24	('777c8a66-eae6-4c4c-8411-1e960acb9087'::uuid,'The correct answer will probably reward us for understanding the causal chain between electrified instruments and fewer people per band, or it''ll test this mathy inference that there are more bands.','Look for an answer that says the number of bands has gone up. A safe answer could also simply restate the chain from electrification to smaller bands, but the math inference about more bands is the one the paragraph is built to test.','Goal','Goal'),
25	('e58b20ed-9724-4eb6-9e88-0a23a49369da'::uuid,'The researcher is offering a mechanism: a slight rise in body temperature lasting until after bedtime causes deeper sleep. Anything that produces that same condition (slight body-temperature rise persisting until bedtime) should produce deeper sleep too — even if it is not exercise.','The study gives us a result: afternoon exercisers got 33 percent more deep sleep than the control group. Then the researcher explains why: afternoon exercise raises body temperature slightly until after bedtime, and "this extra heat induces deeper sleep."
26	
27	That last clause is the key. The researcher does not say exercise itself deepens sleep. The researcher says the extra heat does. So the cause is the heat, and exercise is just one way to produce it. Anything else that leaves you slightly warmer at bedtime should have the same effect.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
28	('9d4092fb-98d3-4c44-aa6a-00bd3cb999c9'::uuid,'Find an answer that the mechanism statement directly supports. Be wary of answers that overstate (e.g., "necessary," "best way," "no one"). 34	
35	Reason (since): it is not clear that the hypothesis could be tested.','Given','Statements'),
36	('863d68ce-2bf8-4261-a390-0d946de21044'::uuid,'Recently, articles criticizing the environmental movement have been appearing regularly in newspapers. 43	('f8806d83-8d1b-400b-bd14-682593ffa525'::uuid,'Engine noise from boats traveling through killer whales’ habitats ranges in frequency from 100 hertz to 3,000 hertz, an acoustical range that overlaps that in which the whales communicate through screams and squeals. 8	('5eb2188f-1b6a-4a72-ada2-eb0666580b82'::uuid,'f9b855cd76ee0307f443d6de6fffc141','The researcher says deeper sleep comes from "this extra heat," a slight rise in body temperature that lasts past bedtime. "Will likely" is as strong as the mechanism allows, and no stronger.'),
9	('397c754d-1182-4f18-b1ee-75711bf8d847'::uuid,'2efd92c19c1cac57841e8c21eaff3fa6','Lacking a test is not evidence against a claim. 10	('99ee3604-856f-40bc-bdef-b640efc8cd6d'::uuid,'ee2f6b4fe7b7869c20790355ad384845','The linguist compares this one hypothesis with physical science. 11	('6cfc6704-6b7f-4152-9d85-d92a1cf9e41c'::uuid,'924b82c414c9871af87cd8c6776bc77b','This turns a fact about the hypothesis into a rule about what we should take seriously. The linguist never says what deserves serious consideration, so nothing here supports dismissing unverifiable hypotheses.'),
12	('3ebae480-2210-4a95-a928-827b5f6fafa9'::uuid,'114632d6222c5c8d190462f93319c0ab','If it is not clear the hypothesis could even be tested, then it has not been confirmed, and we have no reliable way to tell whether it is true. 13	('025294d2-9cc7-496f-8d67-9b1abfdbf33f'::uuid,'698428eb1c27f55c1acb46d9d1ae82fd','Two problems. Like C, it adds a rule about what deserves to be taken seriously, which the linguist never states. 14	('8220c83c-c7af-4f32-a64a-1b18a520b482'::uuid,'8988cdaef5adf28af61f949854b9ec75','The commentator says outright, "It is true that editors like to run antienvironmental pieces mainly because they seem to challenge the political orthodoxy." The move to repeat: when a speaker concedes part of someone''s view and then pivots, sort which part was conceded. The conceded part is safe ground for a Most Supported answer.'),
15	('e5dc97e8-3971-4ead-adb5-2c0e1ffd4858'::uuid,'a58205eff998e12ba0452dba58c706a1','The commentator concedes the opposite: antienvironmentalists "can hardly claim to be dissidents, however much they may have succeeded in selling themselves as renegades." That phrasing grants, or at least allows, that the self-promotion worked. The commentator''s complaint is that the image is false, not that it failed to sell.'),
16	('339b8df5-1dad-4542-a389-3dd84be18520'::uuid,'44fd9f270a5b26c16ba9a0b7bacaec4f','This is the most tempting trap, because the commentator does push back on something. But the commentator agrees that editors run these pieces mainly because they seem to challenge orthodoxy, and that preference fits articles appearing regularly. So Winslow''s explanation does account for the frequency. What the commentator rejects is the idea that the pieces really are daring, which is a different claim from why editors publish them.'),
17	('1e835550-0977-4074-b736-31305a031805'::uuid,'a58205eff998e12ba0452dba58c706a1','The commentator says the reverse: "serious environmentalism is by no means politically orthodox." Environmentalism is the position the critics attack, so the attacked position is not the prevailing one. That is exactly why the critics are not real dissidents.'),
18	('e17f4d50-96b1-4168-bd49-baaa878c30a1'::uuid,'bc6e603214bbf93acea16c77f67836f8','The commentator describes the present: what editors prefer and where environmentalism stands now. Nothing is said about where environmentalism is headed, so a prediction that it will "eventually become" the prevailing position has no support.'),
19	('3a52b47a-b2f8-447f-9c58-a17a5fbd84f0'::uuid,'c83304d38b35f12e57fbb8002b648e50','The only thing we hear about whale behavior around engines is that they "do not seem to behave differently." If anything, that cuts against B, since attempting less communication would be a change in behavior. The premises are about harm to hearing, not about choosing to stay quiet.'),
20	('72aaacd6-11ba-4940-b6e3-8a114451fec2'::uuid,'93e79350d3174acfacae48dc16846258','Both premises meet here. The noise overlaps the frequencies the whales communicate in, and it can damage their hearing. A whale that hears worse in the very range it uses to talk can have trouble communicating. Notice the soft wording: "may impair." The premises say damage "can" happen, so a conclusion that claims only possibility is exactly as strong as the evidence. On a completion question, look for the answer that joins every premise and claims no more than they allow.'),
21	('9b34258e-4710-4eaf-b47c-4b21ba20e83d'::uuid,'d619c4f7bfde1d7e155907aa17256185','The passage never says where whales prefer to be. And it reports that whales do not seem to behave differently around engines, which gives us no sign that they seek out or avoid boat traffic at all.'),
22	('d887952e-572b-4284-8001-a85085e3cad1'::uuid,'04bb98c6e9d6db9f989922724e1025af','Food never comes up. 23	('3c9d564a-c848-4fa0-957c-8db577cc0642'::uuid,'bac8d8429a3d9f1929b03d6876c9b864','The passage tells us where early urban societies could be maintained, not where most people lived. If most early peoples lived in small rural groups that foraged, they could have lived anywhere. A claim about the whole population needs facts about the whole population.'),
24	('dd6262be-992e-4aa7-93d1-b893ed94575b'::uuid,'624490f2fa7bad795281d6a56c3d9d4c','Two problems. First, B is about all societies, but the passage says only that urban ones need large-scale farming; a small foraging society far from water breaks no rule in the passage. Second, B has no time limit, while the passage says irrigation became feasible far from water in more recent times. A modern society far from rivers could farm with irrigation, so B''s "only if" requirement does not follow.'),
25	('a820170c-7235-4d23-887b-828b3b9700cd'::uuid,'2599990c9fede990e0d42db84e9ec444','This follows from combining the three conditional statements, read in early times.
26	
27	Far from rivers or lakes → no irrigation
28	No irrigation → no large-scale farming
29	No large-scale farming → no urban society
30	So: far from rivers or lakes → no urban society
31	
32	The second and third lines are contrapositives of the rules in the stimulus. When a Most Supported paragraph hands you several conditionals that share terms, link them and look for the answer that joins the two ends.'),
33	('da216dbe-f0d1-40d9-b338-aeca5cb51e3f'::uuid,'01e8c2a054fb262f6c7b3dd4a51a5541','The passage points the other way. Early urban societies required large-scale farming, and large-scale farming requires irrigation.
34	
35	Early urban society → large-scale farming → irrigation
36	
37	Being near a river or lake makes irrigation possible; it does not remove the need for it. So D is contradicted, not supported.'),
38	('d6ed2603-9f8e-4766-9559-d4120a6e01e8'::uuid,'ae0b71cf38effc6028c839ec56b08c24','Rural societies never come up. The passage says foraging cannot feed dense urban populations, but it says nothing about how much rural societies foraged compared with how much they farmed.'),
39	('9bc2c618-d490-48e9-93fa-f12d25aae03f'::uuid,'481983aaf965d42e94036df3b41e8528','The passage links decomposition to ammonia smell, not to the percentage of organic matter. 80 percent tells us this is not the best compost, since the best is 40 to 60 percent, but nothing says high organic content means poor decomposition.'),
40	('78fae39e-101e-4beb-929f-0ef124884caa'::uuid,'0053e6447b2c666d923fed39892be0ed','The passage never says any compost harms soil. Compost under 40 percent organic matter and not dark brown fails the description of the best compost, so it is not the best. Not the best is a long way from "will make soil less fertile and will worsen soil drainage." It could still be good compost, or merely unhelpful.'),
41	('9439dc7f-76f2-4532-ac68-b4946cc7ee42'::uuid,'3cae24e9ef7cf9e773fceac928360c8b','This tries to prove a compost is good, and nothing in the passage gives us a way to prove that. The 40 to 60 percent figure is a requirement for the best compost, not a guarantee: best → 40 to 60 percent does not give us 40 to 60 percent → good. And sufficient decomposition only removes one reason to reject a compost. Clearing one obstacle does not make it good.'),
42	('5543b41a-98b9-487a-81e1-8896a6abaf3b'::uuid,'12b1edbb32c9483193c7463532f6f884','We only know that a strong ammonia smell means the organic matter is not sufficiently decomposed. "Sufficiently" is far short of "completely." The passage never describes the decomposition level of the best compost at all.'),
43	('b2448822-0874-41b4-b90f-a5fa28261b6c'::uuid,'5955df3abe1ab7175a8c2e000a3a561d','Chain the first and third statements. Strong ammonia smell → not appropriate for drainage and fertility. Contrapositive of the first rule: not appropriate → not good compost. So smelly compost is not good compost, whatever its color. The dark brown detail is bait: it is a feature of the best compost, but meeting a requirement never proves the label, and the ammonia smell rules the compost out anyway.')

2	('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','No conditional or causal chain here. The useful part is the pivot and its reason. The linguist is not attacking the hypothesis as false. The complaint is about verifiability: it is not clear the hypothesis could be tested at all.
3	
4	What follows from a hypothesis that may not be testable? We cannot confirm it, and we cannot refute it. So we are in no position to say whether it is true. Notice what does not follow: that it is false, or that it should be ignored. The linguist says nothing about either.'),
5	('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a modest answer saying we cannot currently tell whether the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis is true. Expect wrong answers to call it false, or to jump to rules about which hypotheses deserve attention, or to say "only" physical science can be verified.'),
6	('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','This is a concede-then-pivot paragraph, and the hard part is sorting what the commentator accepts from what the commentator rejects.
7	
8	Winslow makes a claim about editors: they publish antienvironmental articles because those articles seem daring, seeming to challenge prevailing positions. The commentator says "It is true" and repeats that point almost word for word. So the commentator accepts Winslow''s account of what editors prefer.
9	
10	Then comes "But." The pivot does not deny the editors'' preference. It denies that the articles actually are daring. Environmentalism is not the orthodox position, so attacking it is not a real challenge to orthodoxy, and the critics are not real dissidents. In short: the articles only seem daring, and that appearance is what editors are buying.
11	
12	On a hard Most Supported question, the conceded part is often where the right answer hides, because the pivot pulls attention away from it.'),
13	('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Winslow is right about the editors: they prefer articles that seem to challenge prevailing positions. Expect traps that treat the "But" as a rejection of Winslow''s whole explanation, that say environmentalism is the orthodox view, or that deny the critics'' success at looking like renegades.'),
14	('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','On a logical completion, the blank is the conclusion, so we ask what the premises are building toward.
15	
16	Look for the overlap. Premise one connects engine noise to communication: the noise sits in the whales'' communication range. Premise two connects engine noise to hearing damage. Put them together: boat noise can damage the very hearing the whales use to communicate.
17	
18	The "Though" clause matters too. The whales do not act differently around engines, which might make us think the noise is harmless. The author is warning against that: no visible reaction does not mean no harm.'),
19	('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a conclusion like "boat engine noise may harm the whales'' ability to communicate, even though they show no reaction." Keep it soft, since the premises only say the damage "can" happen, and keep it tied to communication.'),
20	('db0144e4-130f-4db7-bd8d-55643f882b80'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','Three conditionals, and they share terms: large-scale farming appears in the first and second rules, irrigation in the second and third. Shared terms are a signal to chain.
21	
22	Early urban society → large-scale farming → irrigation
23	
24	Now look at the irrigation fact. Far from rivers or lakes, early irrigation was not feasible. Run the chain backward with contrapositives: no irrigation means no large-scale farming, and no large-scale farming means no early urban society.
25	
26	Keep the time limit in view. Irrigation became feasible far from water "in more recent times," so the conclusion only holds for early times.'),
27	('db0144e4-130f-4db7-bd8d-55643f882b80'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying that, in early times, urban societies could not be maintained far from rivers or lakes. Watch for answers that drop "early," drop "urban," or claim anything about where people actually lived.'),
28	('3271c974-3514-4332-a22a-1f337613f0ba'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','Two rules talk about the same thing: whether compost may be used for drainage and fertility.
29	
30	Good compost → appropriate for drainage and fertility
31	Strong ammonia smell → not appropriate for drainage and fertility
32	
33	Take the contrapositive of the first rule: not appropriate → not good compost. Now chain:
34	
35	Strong ammonia smell → not appropriate → not good compost
36	
37	So any compost with a strong ammonia smell is not good compost, no matter what else is true of it.
38	
39	The best-compost sentence is a distraction. It gives requirements for the best compost, so it can only tell us when something is not the best. It can never prove a compost is good or best.'),
40	('3271c974-3514-4332-a22a-1f337613f0ba'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying that compost with a strong ammonia smell is not good compost. Expect wrong answers that use the 40 to 60 percent or dark brown features to prove a compost is good, or that invent a link between organic percentage and decomposition.')
41	) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;

{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values\n('7354eaa5-1294-49df-ab0f-3458e9be7389'::uuid,'a8df74d4e3c1598e7fe7b419b4259fa3','The paragraph only counts musicians who play popular music professionally. Amateurs never come up, so we have no information about whether their numbers rose, fell, or stayed the same.'),\n('962b0b9d-9835-47ba-9e0a-02e0921495bc'::uuid,'febd2ef5b90fcf03ac163ea00160288e','The paragraph says instruments were electrified. It never says whether any musician can play a nonelectric instrument, let alone most of them. A claim about musicians'' skills has nothing to stand on here.'),\n('d91af5c4-50d1-42f6-a8d5-9de7ed44243e'::uuid,'a309023ba27a9c0e7ef76b6afa929aab','This is one way to spread musicians across more bands, but nothing in the paragraph says anyone plays in more than one band. The math only forces more bands; it does not tell us how musicians are spread across them. Every musician could play in exactly one band and the facts still hold.'),\n('9c0ce691-0918-4027-bfba-0f5ea6a58ebc'::uuid,'01d4c48b2758d3e1d13163c448a3fd1f','\"Required\" means no one gets adequate deep sleep without afternoon exercise. The study only shows that exercisers got more deep sleep than a group that exercised little or not at all. And the mechanism points the other way: if heat is the cause, other sources of heat could do the job without exercise.'),\n('1ebea67e-4423-4cf3-aa35-c5e3edd1e656'::uuid,'6268c37712c42a54c672f6ef4f46375b','The mechanism depends on body heat lasting until after bedtime. Morning exercise never comes up, and its heat could easily wear off long before night. Nothing lets us compare morning exercise with afternoon exercise.'),\n('db0dc54b-4fa7-444a-8f01-1804375a5c5d'::uuid,'a61e3cee6299c2a5d3d18a78e07f95ac','\"The best way\" needs a comparison with every other way to get deep sleep. The researcher describes one thing that helps. That supports \"this works,\" not \"nothing works better.\"'),\n('5eb2188f-1b6a-4a72-ada2-eb0666580b82'::uuid,'f9b855cd76ee0307f443d6de6fffc141','The researcher says deeper sleep comes from \"this extra heat,\" a slight rise in body temperature that lasts past bedtime. A warm bath just before bed produces that same condition, so the mechanism predicts it should help too. Notice the move: when a stimulus names the mechanism behind a result, the supported answer often applies that mechanism to a new case. \"Will likely\" is as strong as the mechanism allows, and no stronger.'),\n('397c754d-1182-4f18-b1ee-75711bf8d847'::uuid,'2efd92c19c1cac57841e8c21eaff3fa6','Lacking a test is not evidence against a claim. The linguist says we may not be able to check the hypothesis, which leaves it unconfirmed, not probably false. If we cannot test something, we have no grounds to call it likely true or likely false.'),\n('99ee3604-856f-40bc-bdef-b640efc8cd6d'::uuid,'ee2f6b4fe7b7869c20790355ad384845','The linguist compares this one hypothesis with physical science. That tells us physical-science hypotheses are verifiable, not that they are the only verifiable ones. Plenty of hypotheses outside physical science could be testable; the passage just never discusses them.'),\n('6cfc6704-6b7f-4152-9d85-d92a1cf9e41c'::uuid,'924b82c414c9871af87cd8c6776bc77b','This turns a fact about the hypothesis into a rule about what we should take seriously. The linguist never says what deserves serious consideration, so nothing here supports dismissing unverifiable hypotheses.'),\n('3ebae480-2210-4a95-a928-827b5f6fafa9'::uuid,'114632d6222c5c8d190462f93319c0ab','If it is not clear the hypothesis could even be tested, then it has not been confirmed, and we have no reliable way to tell whether it is true. That leaves us exactly where D says: we do not know. Notice how little D claims. It takes the linguist''s point about testing one small step, which is what a supported answer usually does.'),\n('025294d2-9cc7-496f-8d67-9b1abfdbf33f'::uuid,'698428eb1c27f55c1acb46d9d1ae82fd','Two problems. Like C, it adds a rule about what deserves to be taken seriously, which the linguist never states. And like B, it says \"only\" physical science, when the passage compares one hypothesis with physical science and says nothing about any other field.'),\n('8220c83c-c7af-4f32-a64a-1b18a520b482'::uuid,'8988cdaef5adf28af61f949854b9ec75','The commentator says outright, \"It is true that editors like to run antienvironmental pieces mainly because they seem to challenge the political orthodoxy.\" That is Winslow''s claim about editors, confirmed. The \"But\" that follows attacks the articles, not Winslow''s account of editors. The move to repeat: when a speaker concedes part of someone''s view and then pivots, sort which part was conceded. The conceded part is safe ground for a Most Supported answer.'),\n('e5dc97e8-3971-4ead-adb5-2c0e1ffd4858'::uuid,'a58205eff998e12ba0452dba58c706a1','The commentator concedes the opposite: antienvironmentalists \"can hardly claim to be dissidents, however much they may have succeeded in selling themselves as renegades.\" That phrasing grants, or at least allows, that the self-promotion worked. The commentator''s complaint is that the image is false, not that it failed to sell.'),\n('339b8df5-1dad-4542-a389-3dd84be18520'::uuid,'44fd9f270a5b26c16ba9a0b7bacaec4f','This is the most tempting trap, because the commentator does push back on something. But the commentator agrees that editors run these pieces mainly because they seem to challenge orthodoxy, and that preference fits articles appearing regularly. So Winslow''s explanation does account for the frequency. What the commentator rejects is the idea that the pieces really are daring, which is a different claim from why editors publish them.'),\n('1e835550-0977-4074-b736-31305a031805'::uuid,'a58205eff998e12ba0452dba58c706a1','The commentator says the reverse: \"serious environmentalism is by no means politically orthodox.\" Environmentalism is the position the critics attack, so the attacked position is not the prevailing one. That is exactly why the critics are not real dissidents.'),\n('e17f4d50-96b1-4168-bd49-baaa878c30a1'::uuid,'bc6e603214bbf93acea16c77f67836f8','The commentator describes the present: what editors prefer and where environmentalism stands now. Nothing is said about where environmentalism is headed, so a prediction that it will \"eventually become\" the prevailing position has no support.'),\n('3a52b47a-b2f8-447f-9c58-a17a5fbd84f0'::uuid,'c83304d38b35f12e57fbb8002b648e50','The only thing we hear about whale behavior around engines is that they \"do not seem to behave differently.\" If anything, that cuts against B, since attempting less communication would be a change in behavior. The premises are about harm to hearing, not about choosing to stay quiet.'),\n('72aaacd6-11ba-4940-b6e3-8a114451fec2'::uuid,'93e79350d3174acfacae48dc16846258','Both premises meet here. The noise overlaps the frequencies the whales communicate in, and it can damage their hearing. A whale that hears worse in the very range it uses to talk can have trouble communicating. Notice the soft wording: \"may impair.\" The premises say damage \"can\" happen, so a conclusion that claims only possibility is exactly as strong as the evidence. On a completion question, look for the answer that joins every premise and claims no more than they allow.'),\n('9b34258e-4710-4eaf-b47c-4b21ba20e83d'::uuid,'d619c4f7bfde1d7e155907aa17256185','The passage never says where whales prefer to be. And it reports that whales do not seem to behave differently around engines, which gives us no sign that they seek out or avoid boat traffic at all.'),\n('d887952e-572b-4284-8001-a85085e3cad1'::uuid,'04bb98c6e9d6db9f989922724e1025af','Food never comes up. To get here we would need to know the whales use hearing to hunt, and the passage only ties their hearing to communication. \"Would probably be more successful\" also claims an effect the premises never measure.'),\n('3c9d564a-c848-4fa0-957c-8db577cc0642'::uuid,'bac8d8429a3d9f1929b03d6876c9b864','The passage tells us where early urban societies could be maintained, not where most people lived. If most early peoples lived in small rural groups that foraged, they could have lived anywhere. A claim about the whole population needs facts about the whole population.'),\n('dd6262be-992e-4aa7-93d1-b893ed94575b'::uuid,'624490f2fa7bad795281d6a56c3d9d4c','Two problems. First, B is about all societies, but the passage says only that urban ones need large-scale farming; a small foraging society far from water breaks no rule in the passage. Second, B has no time limit, while the passage says irrigation became feasible far from water in more recent times. A modern society far from rivers could farm with irrigation, so B''s \"only if\" requirement does not follow.'),\n('a820170c-7235-4d23-887b-828b3b9700cd'::uuid,'2599990c9fede990e0d42db84e9ec444','This follows from combining the three conditional statements, read in early times.\n\nFar from rivers or lakes → no irrigation\nNo irrigation → no large-scale farming\nNo large-scale farming → no urban society\nSo: far from rivers or lakes → no urban society\n\nThe second and third lines are contrapositives of the rules in the stimulus. When a Most Supported paragraph hands you several conditionals that share terms, link them and look for the answer that joins the two ends.'),\n('da216dbe-f0d1-40d9-b338-aeca5cb51e3f'::uuid,'01e8c2a054fb262f6c7b3dd4a51a5541','The passage points the other way. Early urban societies required large-scale farming, and large-scale farming requires irrigation.\n\nEarly urban society → large-scale farming → irrigation\n\nBeing near a river or lake makes irrigation possible; it does not remove the need for it. So D is contradicted, not supported.'),\n('d6ed2603-9f8e-4766-9559-d4120a6e01e8'::uuid,'ae0b71cf38effc6028c839ec56b08c24','Rural societies never come up. The passage says foraging cannot feed dense urban populations, but it says nothing about how much rural societies foraged compared with how much they farmed.'),\n('9bc2c618-d490-48e9-93fa-f12d25aae03f'::uuid,'481983aaf965d42e94036df3b41e8528','The passage links decomposition to ammonia smell, not to the percentage of organic matter. 80 percent tells us this is not the best compost, since the best is 40 to 60 percent, but nothing says high organic content means poor decomposition.'),\n('78fae39e-101e-4beb-929f-0ef124884caa'::uuid,'0053e6447b2c666d923fed39892be0ed','The passage never says any compost harms soil. Compost under 40 percent organic matter and not dark brown fails the description of the best compost, so it is not the best. Not the best is a long way from \"will make soil less fertile and will worsen soil drainage.\" It could still be good compost, or merely unhelpful.'),\n('9439dc7f-76f2-4532-ac68-b4946cc7ee42'::uuid,'3cae24e9ef7cf9e773fceac928360c8b','This tries to prove a compost is good, and nothing in the passage gives us a way to prove that. The 40 to 60 percent figure is a requirement for the best compost, not a guarantee: best → 40 to 60 percent does not give us 40 to 60 percent → good. And sufficient decomposition only removes one reason to reject a compost. Clearing one obstacle does not make it good.'),\n('5543b41a-98b9-487a-81e1-8896a6abaf3b'::uuid,'12b1edbb32c9483193c7463532f6f884','We only know that a strong ammonia smell means the organic matter is not sufficiently decomposed. \"Sufficiently\" is far short of \"completely.\" The passage never describes the decomposition level of the best compost at all.'),\n('b2448822-0874-41b4-b90f-a5fa28261b6c'::uuid,'5955df3abe1ab7175a8c2e000a3a561d','Chain the first and third statements. Strong ammonia smell → not appropriate for drainage and fertility. Contrapositive of the first rule: not appropriate → not good compost. So smelly compost is not good compost, whatever its color. The dark brown detail is bait: it is a feature of the best compost, but meeting a requirement never proves the label, and the ammonia smell rules the compost out anyway.')\n) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;"}

{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\n('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','No conditional or causal chain here. The useful part is the pivot and its reason. The linguist is not attacking the hypothesis as false. The complaint is about verifiability: it is not clear the hypothesis could be tested at all.\n\nWhat follows from a hypothesis that may not be testable? We cannot confirm it, and we cannot refute it. So we are in no position to say whether it is true. Notice what does not follow: that it is false, or that it should be ignored. The linguist says nothing about either.'),\n('87919b24-e7fb-42a1-b12d-7f8ce5f8d72e'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a modest answer saying we cannot currently tell whether the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis is true. Expect wrong answers to call it false, or to jump to rules about which hypotheses deserve attention, or to say \"only\" physical science can be verified.'),\n('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','This is a concede-then-pivot paragraph, and the hard part is sorting what the commentator accepts from what the commentator rejects.\n\nWinslow makes a claim about editors: they publish antienvironmental articles because those articles seem daring, seeming to challenge prevailing positions. The commentator says \"It is true\" and repeats that point almost word for word. So the commentator accepts Winslow''s account of what editors prefer.\n\nThen comes \"But.\" The pivot does not deny the editors'' preference. It denies that the articles actually are daring. Environmentalism is not the orthodox position, so attacking it is not a real challenge to orthodoxy, and the critics are not real dissidents. In short: the articles only seem daring, and that appearance is what editors are buying.\n\nOn a hard Most Supported question, the conceded part is often where the right answer hides, because the pivot pulls attention away from it.'),\n('979d1f49-6d4d-4f2a-a294-59fd314c0fbd'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Winslow is right about the editors: they prefer articles that seem to challenge prevailing positions. Expect traps that treat the \"But\" as a rejection of Winslow''s whole explanation, that say environmentalism is the orthodox view, or that deny the critics'' success at looking like renegades.'),\n('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','On a logical completion, the blank is the conclusion, so we ask what the premises are building toward.\n\nLook for the overlap. Premise one connects engine noise to communication: the noise sits in the whales'' communication range. Premise two connects engine noise to hearing damage. Put them together: boat noise can damage the very hearing the whales use to communicate.\n\nThe \"Though\" clause matters too. The whales do not act differently around engines, which might make us think the noise is harmless. The author is warning against that: no visible reaction does not mean no harm.'),\n('81205ffa-816c-4c4f-8466-43873dfa8ead'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for a conclusion like \"boat engine noise may harm the whales'' ability to communicate, even though they show no reaction.\" Keep it soft, since the premises only say the damage \"can\" happen, and keep it tied to communication.'),\n('db0144e4-130f-4db7-bd8d-55643f882b80'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','Three conditionals, and they share terms: large-scale farming appears in the first and second rules, irrigation in the second and third. Shared terms are a signal to chain.\n\nEarly urban society → large-scale farming → irrigation\n\nNow look at the irrigation fact. Far from rivers or lakes, early irrigation was not feasible. Run the chain backward with contrapositives: no irrigation means no large-scale farming, and no large-scale farming means no early urban society.\n\nKeep the time limit in view. Irrigation became feasible far from water \"in more recent times,\" so the conclusion only holds for early times.'),\n('db0144e4-130f-4db7-bd8d-55643f882b80'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying that, in early times, urban societies could not be maintained far from rivers or lakes. Watch for answers that drop \"early,\" drop \"urban,\" or claim anything about where people actually lived.'),\n('3271c974-3514-4332-a22a-1f337613f0ba'::uuid,1,'Evaluate','Two rules talk about the same thing: whether compost may be used for drainage and fertility.\n\nGood compost → appropriate for drainage and fertility\nStrong ammonia smell → not appropriate for drainage and fertility\n\nTake the contrapositive of the first rule: not appropriate → not good compost. Now chain:\n\nStrong ammonia smell → not appropriate → not good compost\n\nSo any compost with a strong ammonia smell is not good compost, no matter what else is true of it.\n\nThe best-compost sentence is a distraction. It gives requirements for the best compost, so it can only tell us when something is not the best. It can never prove a compost is good or best.'),\n('3271c974-3514-4332-a22a-1f337613f0ba'::uuid,2,'Goal','Look for an answer saying that compost with a strong ammonia smell is not good compost. Expect wrong answers that use the 40 to 60 percent or dark brown features to prove a compost is good, or that invent a link between organic percentage and decomposition.')\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;"}

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('b13f2d7f-892c-438a-a4db-4433f79e009e'::uuid,'be6d8383a6695a6e0ad133cc9202a95a','Go claim by claim through Paula''s argument and ask of each one: does Ashley push back?
3	
4	1. Earthlike planets are common, so there probably are intelligent beings on other planets. Ashley never touches this. Calling the search a waste doesn''t mean nothing is out there. It can just mean we''re listening for the wrong signal.
5	
6	2. Alien scientists would understand math and physics the way we do, so they would inevitably develop technologies such as radio communication. Ashley rejects this directly. Our science, mathematics, and technology are "unique outgrowths" of our physiology, cognitive makeup, and environment. Different beings, different outgrowths. The legal and political systems comparison is there to make Paula''s "inevitably" sound as odd as expecting aliens to have our courts and juries.
7	
8	3. We can expect our radio telescopes to detect signs of such beings. Ashley rejects this too: the search is "a waste of time and money."
9	
10	So the friction sits in claims 2 and 3, and they share one core question: would intelligent aliens end up with radio? Paula says inevitably. Ashley says there''s no reason to expect it.
11	
12	Underneath, Paula treats math and physics as something any intelligent mind would arrive at. Ashley treats them as a product of being human.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
13	('d03bb8ee-aa3f-48a6-bbe2-12ed75b7372a'::uuid,'725626240c027adec99de8fb27a5d15d','Look for an answer about whether intelligent aliens would have developed radio communication, or, nearly the same thing, whether radio telescopes are a sensible way to look for them. Paula has to land on yes and Ashley on no.
14	
15	Be careful with answers about intelligent life alone. Ashley never says whether aliens exist, so an answer about existence gives us an Agree position from Paula and nothing from Ashley.','Goal','Goal'),
16	('48e638a9-3a7e-490f-b2af-8569e906daf9'::uuid,'4bb6ba9ee22d1a012843172a1e94151f','Archaeologists can study the artifacts left by ancient cultures to determine whether they were nomadic or sedentary.
17	
18	Does Kendall push back on this? Yes, at least partly. Kendall says what artifacts a people make is determined largely by the materials available to them. If materials drive what gets made, the artifacts may tell us more about the local supply of stone and wood than about whether the people moved around. That makes the artifact test much less reliable.','Robin''s 1st Claim','Robin''s 1st Claim'),
19	('24ec064d-ffc5-4f19-b19f-d7442d3655c0'::uuid,'3985510ed1ddcfe30e9c3382a7fd8025','If artifacts were made to last, rather than to be quickly discarded, then the culture was likely sedentary.
20	
21	Does Kendall deny this exact claim? Not cleanly. Kendall''s point lands hardest on the flip side. Picture a settled village with nothing around it but reeds and soft wood. Everything it makes falls apart within a few years. Robin''s test would call that village nomadic, and Kendall''s point explains why that would be a mistake: the flimsy artifacts reflect the materials, not a life on the move.
22	
23	So Kendall might accept "made to last, so probably sedentary" while rejecting "not durable, so probably nomadic."','Robin''s 2nd Claim','Robin''s 2nd Claim'),
24	('eb15399b-1322-4d8c-88f7-888ae61f1933'::uuid,'189655fed1636dee9a9a7abcbb23ae0a','Robin is committed to more than the durable half. The first claim says artifacts can determine whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary, so Robin''s test has to sort both ways: lasting artifacts point to sedentary, throwaway artifacts point to nomadic.
25	
26	Kendall''s response targets that second half. Look for an answer about whether non-durable artifacts give us good reason to call a culture nomadic. Robin says yes. Kendall says no, because the available materials may explain the flimsiness.','Goal','Goal'),
27	('dfb3414b-7299-447e-929f-c89a5f9aa85b'::uuid,'a0cce756d582481a87367b7d748ab388','A few months into a high-salt diet, 75% of the rats in the colony had high or extremely high blood pressure; only 25% had normal blood pressure.','Evidence','Evidence'),
28	('ebb4113c-41f0-41e3-8cd2-3520cf6cbd26'::uuid,'1ce47db39da0058b2ccb3f0e1d489af6','The study has a result but no starting point. It measured blood pressure a few months into the diet, and that''s all we get.
29	
30	So ask: what if most of these rats already had high blood pressure before the study began? Then 75% at the end tells us nothing about salt. The diet didn''t move the numbers. It just sat on top of them.
31	
32	Now flip it. If almost every rat started out normal and 75% ended up high, the diet looks much more like the culprit.
33	
34	Whenever an argument draws a link from an after-measurement alone, the missing piece is the before-measurement, or a comparison group that never got the treatment.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
35	('2e41c9fa-3934-4afd-8862-c33846c4a63a'::uuid,'ca830a23f2d74e1ba57c9e2ee01f8d43','Look for a question about the rats'' blood pressure before the diet started.
36	
37	Test each choice by imagining two different answers to it. If one answer would help the conclusion and the other would hurt it, the question is relevant. If every possible answer leaves the conclusion where it was, move on.','Goal','Goal'),
38	('bfbb2fa9-f17a-41dd-ae12-f53af0c9af03'::uuid,'28c0a6de536d01b1be5a3adc5d5c5b42','If we''re the other lawyer and we''re arguing,
39	*there IS some reason not to try this herb juice*
40	what would we say?
41	  We could start with, "Wait. Some doctors said the herbs are potentially harmful? That sounds like a reason not to try it."
42	  And the herbalist will say, "Oh, don''t listen to them. Doctors don''t want herbs to work. They want a monopoly over medical therapies, so they talk down anything that isn''t theirs."
43	  We need a response. We could say, "Sure, doctors might have a motive. But a motive doesn''t make the warning false. What if the herbs actually are harmful? Do you have evidence they''re safe?"
44	  If we know our Famous Flaws, we''ll recognize this as a classic Ad Hominem. That''s when the author dismisses a claim because of who is making it, usually by pointing to (1) an ulterior motive or vested interest, or (2) hypocrisy or conflicting past behavior.
45	  Here, the herbalist disqualifies the doctors'' warning just because doctors supposedly have an interest in herbal treatments looking bad. The warning itself never gets answered.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
46	('1c0d8000-8f52-451a-8497-28414f832400'::uuid,'52bb1c2ae7a618e8df751171a28af913','Look for an answer saying the herbalist dismisses the doctors'' claim by attacking the doctors'' motives instead of addressing whether the herbs are harmful.
47	  Or, there might be an answer saying, "even if the herb juice isn''t harmful, there still might be some *other* reason not to try it" (its price, its taste, or the time it takes away from a treatment that works).','Goal','Goal'),
48	('bdf0a819-b92d-4f0e-9717-bb131e5adfae'::uuid,'e8d4ddf38d804d0ec92d7385775f3848','The argument gives us two facts that share a middle term:
49	
50	creativity → theta waves increase
51	music → theta waves increase
52	
53	Then it concludes that music produces creativity. But the first fact only says creativity comes with more theta waves. It doesn''t say more theta waves bring creativity. That would be the reversal:
54	
55	theta waves increase → creativity
56	
57	So how could we argue against the conclusion? Suppose music raises theta waves and that''s all it does. Nothing in the evidence rules out theta waves rising with no creativity at all. Then music could raise theta waves without producing creativity.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
58	('1e6e5a48-b204-4ff2-b652-3f7d6b427983'::uuid,'a1648b9ec77c3fca851ad692f7d1ccc9','Find an answer pointing out that theta waves can increase without a state of profound creativity. In other words, the argument treats something that accompanies creativity as if it were enough to produce creativity.','Goal','Goal'),
59	('0ffd2b3d-74c4-41d5-8bda-ee53fe3518d0'::uuid,'1aae6036fd45f0d5d09858fc925c25d8','The figures our computer program provides are remarkably consistent from week to week.','Evidence','Evidence'),
60	('274cdce0-e580-4603-ada2-7483a127bd9e'::uuid,'6a839c6ab7c188b9d36f9313030ed680','We can be confident of its accuracy.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),
61	('508136f4-1fb5-4f54-a32a-ff670e870566'::uuid,'d5411b60bd964e793c13d942b8bc7d19','Beck moves from consistent to accurate:
62	
63	consistent → accurate
64	
65	How could we argue the other side? Picture a bathroom scale that always reads five pounds too heavy. Your weight hasn''t changed, so you get the same number every morning. Perfectly consistent, and wrong every time.
66	
67	The program could be like that scale. If it makes the same mistake every week, its figures would be consistent and inaccurate. Consistency tells us the program is stable, not that it''s right.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
68	('243e6ec7-ea2e-4f77-b29b-db10400a82d9'::uuid,'e73aeb52b502f22022138afddf824510','Look at the timing. The consent forms are filled out before the test. The resentment the ethicists describe comes after: when a subject learns they got a placebo, or that the medicine didn''t work.
69	
70	How could we argue that many subjects still resent it? Accepting a risk isn''t the same as being fine with how it turns out. You can agree to a coin flip and still be upset when you lose. These subjects are suffering from the diseases being treated and hoping the medicine helps. Signing a form that says "I understand I might get a placebo" fits perfectly well with resentment when the placebo arrives.
71	
72	So the columnist assumes the attitude on the form is the attitude after the outcome.','Evaluate','Evaluate')
73	) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;

The warning itself never gets answered.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('1c0d8000-8f52-451a-8497-28414f832400'::uuid,'52bb1c2ae7a618e8df751171a28af913','Look for an answer saying the herbalist dismisses the doctors'' claim by attacking the doctors'' motives instead of addressing whether the herbs are harmful.\n  Or, there might be an answer saying, \"even if the herb juice isn''t harmful, there still might be some *other* reason not to try it\" (its price, its taste, or the time it takes away from a treatment that works).','Goal','Goal'),\n('bdf0a819-b92d-4f0e-9717-bb131e5adfae'::uuid,'e8d4ddf38d804d0ec92d7385775f3848','The argument gives us two facts that share a middle term:\n\ncreativity → theta waves increase\nmusic → theta waves increase\n\nThen it concludes that music produces creativity. But the first fact only says creativity comes with more theta waves. It doesn''t say more theta waves bring creativity. That would be the reversal:\n\ntheta waves increase → creativity\n\nSo how could we argue against the conclusion? Suppose music raises theta waves and that''s all it does. Nothing in the evidence rules out theta waves rising with no creativity at all. Then music could raise theta waves without producing creativity.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('1e6e5a48-b204-4ff2-b652-3f7d6b427983'::uuid,'a1648b9ec77c3fca851ad692f7d1ccc9','Find an answer pointing out that theta waves can increase without a state of profound creativity. In other words, the argument treats something that accompanies creativity as if it were enough to produce creativity.','Goal','Goal'),\n('0ffd2b3d-74c4-41d5-8bda-ee53fe3518d0'::uuid,'1aae6036fd45f0d5d09858fc925c25d8','The figures our computer program provides are remarkably consistent from week to week.','Evidence','Evidence'),\n('274cdce0-e580-4603-ada2-7483a127bd9e'::uuid,'6a839c6ab7c188b9d36f9313030ed680','We can be confident of its accuracy.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),\n('508136f4-1fb5-4f54-a32a-ff670e870566'::uuid,'d5411b60bd964e793c13d942b8bc7d19','Beck moves from consistent to accurate:\n\nconsistent → accurate\n\nHow could we argue the other side? Picture a bathroom scale that always reads five pounds too heavy. Your weight hasn''t changed, so you get the same number every morning. Perfectly consistent, and wrong every time.\n\nThe program could be like that scale. If it makes the same mistake every week, its figures would be consistent and inaccurate. Consistency tells us the program is stable, not that it''s right.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),\n('243e6ec7-ea2e-4f77-b29b-db10400a82d9'::uuid,'e73aeb52b502f22022138afddf824510','Look at the timing. The consent forms are filled out before the test. The resentment the ethicists describe comes after: when a subject learns they got a placebo, or that the medicine didn''t work.\n\nHow could we argue that many subjects still resent it? Accepting a risk isn''t the same as being fine with how it turns out. You can agree to a coin flip and still be upset when you lose. These subjects are suffering from the diseases being treated and hoping the medicine helps. Signing a form that says \"I understand I might get a placebo\" fits perfectly well with resentment when the placebo arrives.\n\nSo the columnist assumes the attitude on the form is the attitude after the outcome.','Evaluate','Evaluate')\n) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;"}

1	update lr_explanation_choice_notes t set body=v.nb from (values
2	('910ca695-c885-4ed5-9bb8-2c4c9e249e5e'::uuid,'980807a53b1c94b0f48190d544225874','This combines the two claims Ashley attacks. Paula says there probably are intelligent beings on other planets and that they would "inevitably" develop technologies "such as radio communication." So Paula agrees it is probable that such beings exist and have radio.
3	
4	Ashley disagrees. If our technology is a unique outgrowth of human physiology, cognitive makeup, and environment, there''s no reason to expect aliens to have radio. That''s exactly why Ashley calls the radio-telescope search a waste.
5	
6	The move to repeat: find the claim the second speaker actually attacks, then pick the answer that restates it. Here that''s radio, not alien life itself.'),
7	('3d1872e8-5096-4a7b-ae9d-e8b6ff0b126e'::uuid,'675189f3a3d47dc6d13dd8ad88c2a14b','Paula says nothing about alien legal or political systems, so there''s no Agree position. Ashley raises them only as a comparison: assuming aliens share our physics is as unjustified as assuming they share our laws. A disagreement needs both speakers on record, and Paula is silent here.'),
8	('c08c751b-64fc-4535-acc4-b89cc0ce9b41'::uuid,'accd0a5d7d096c5eca0e53617ca03d46','Ashley agrees: technology is an "outgrowth" of our cognitive makeup. But can we find a Disagree position from Paula? No. Paula''s claim is about an outcome, that aliens would reach radio. Paula could grant that cognitive makeup influences technology and still think any intelligent species would get to radio anyway.'),
9	('59486f59-4475-4f97-8488-483d043fdbb5'::uuid,'086ee8a24f7f0072c378f9c74b5704fc','This is the tempting one, because Paula clearly agrees: there "probably are intelligent beings on other planets." Now look for Ashley''s Disagree position. Ashley attacks the method, radio telescopes, and the claim that aliens would share our science. Nothing Ashley says denies that intelligent life exists. Ashley''s objection makes perfect sense even if the aliens are out there and simply never built radios. With no Disagree position, D fails.'),
10	('418051c8-0731-4c10-af35-1233f66a84c7'::uuid,'dd9d9997b1b28646b846ff5d031f82b4','Paula never says scientists should spend more. Paula expects the telescopes to detect signs of aliens, which is a prediction, not a call for more funding, so there''s no Agree position. Ashley''s side is shaky too: calling a radio search "a waste of time and money" rejects one method, and Ashley could still back other ways of searching. Neither speaker is clearly on record.'),
11	('604339a1-b41d-4bc1-a2ec-5548d1327a84'::uuid,'313bd49d4287b88498bd3cb69ab2dfbc','Robin plainly accepts the nomadic/sedentary distinction. Does Kendall call it illicit? No. Kendall never says the categories are confused or improper, only that artifacts are a shaky way to sort cultures into them. Doubting a test is not the same as rejecting the categories it sorts into, so there''s no Disagree position.'),
12	('4523229d-3b31-4f8e-9b66-0b471170567c'::uuid,'872a850cb2fd67284c7838f5ac521ef7','Robin agrees. The first claim says artifacts can tell us whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary, and the second ties lasting artifacts to sedentary life. Put together, artifacts that were not made to last point toward nomadic.
13	
14	Kendall disagrees. If materials largely determine what a people make, a settled culture with only flimsy materials would leave non-durable artifacts too. Then assuming "nomadic" from non-durable artifacts isn''t reasonable.
15	
16	Notice that B isn''t Robin''s second claim word for word. It''s the other half of the test that Robin''s first claim commits Robin to, and it''s the half Kendall''s point actually breaks. Ask which claim the second speaker''s objection would make false, then match that claim.'),
17	('297d85c3-9a3c-4ce6-8cfc-b679b18393f1'::uuid,'f3c53bff609edebec1208102d3b30475','C asks whether evidence other than durability can establish a culture''s type conclusively. Robin never says durability is the only evidence, and Robin''s own claim is hedged with "likely." Kendall never mentions other evidence at all. With neither speaker on record, there''s nothing to disagree about.'),
18	('d83eee86-31b4-41da-8964-4fb4fd46f5a3'::uuid,'33662ddddafd89ee741e9170ad08b902','D brings in what "many archaeologists have thought" about the distinction''s importance. Neither speaker mentions other archaeologists or ranks how important the distinction is. Robin uses the distinction; Kendall questions one way of detecting it. Neither position is supported.'),
19	('51760e4b-3637-4f26-ab1b-f94b788805c2'::uuid,'ab1915666a580c6588b207b891a06df2','Robin agrees: artifacts can reveal whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary. Does Kendall disagree? Kendall says artifacts reflect the materials available, which is itself something artifacts reveal. Kendall doubts one inference, not the value of studying artifacts. Rejecting one use of the evidence isn''t saying the evidence reveals little, so there''s no Disagree position.'),
20	('5201a04c-8111-4353-93d9-b6599f6c1a52'::uuid,'aedcd5677b0cdd694452bc2effb6948a','Imagine two answers: twice the normal salt, or ten times. Either way, we still don''t know whether the rats had high blood pressure before the study. The amount of salt tells us how big the dose was, not whether the dose did anything.'),
21	('cb25f9bc-5c54-402b-b304-cef5fa0449e6'::uuid,'b8294f555e5d4ec249d3bb6fbffc0c53','Suppose the high blood pressure caused serious harm, or none at all. The conclusion is only that high-salt diets are linked to high blood pressure. What high blood pressure does afterward can''t tell us whether salt produced it.'),
22	('ea79abb1-e1f7-4376-b6f9-6235bf63eb65'::uuid,'e2b8ec31a54bf328f29b914afc454eea','How many wild colonies eat salty food tells us how common the diet is, not what it does. Whether the answer is 1 percent or 90 percent, this study''s evidence for a link is exactly as strong as before.'),
23	('64884a51-d794-4886-84ae-ed3ef63e7e32'::uuid,'837d07997b4dadba38fa76ebfbd041c1','This is the before-measurement the study is missing. Run both answers. If most of the colony already had abnormally high blood pressure, the after-numbers show nothing about salt and the conclusion falls apart. If almost none did, a jump to 75 percent points straight at the diet.
24	
25	One question, two answers, opposite effects on the conclusion: that''s what a relevant Evaluate question does. Next time a study reports only results after a treatment, ask what the numbers were before it.'),
26	('834a2eeb-c931-4942-b72b-43979984981e'::uuid,'7f336a650ce87d814c2baeebbaa9a7ee','Yes or no, other rodent experiments say nothing about this colony. Even a yes wouldn''t tell us what those experiments found, and the conclusion is about rats anyway.'),
27	('d0e37fc7-a4b8-4a25-b6d4-ac1d0ed28730'::uuid,'38ebfc79eb9abf751795db7d5d98a4dc','This is the appeal-to-fear flaw: accept my claim, or something bad will happen to you. The herbalist never threatens anyone with consequences for skipping the juice. If anyone raised a fear, it was the doctors, and the herbalist is the one brushing that fear aside.'),
28	('e2ee319d-f723-40e0-9f20-44493b7aeb85'::uuid,'9c67ce0540b7fc3d8dda407410a41ba7','Check the premises against each other. Customers report better coordination; a few doctors say the herbs are potentially harmful. Both can be true at once: something can help your coordination and still carry a risk. No contradiction, so this flaw isn''t here.'),
29	('ae200272-4dfb-4865-a572-40b509bbc532'::uuid,'eb3e2b89749cfce9014c7c30f45f325c','The doctors claim the herbs are potentially harmful. The herbalist never says why that''s wrong. Instead, the herbalist points out that doctors want a monopoly, then concludes there''s no reason not to try the juice. A motive to make a claim doesn''t show the claim is false. A doctor with a monopoly to protect can still be right about a harmful herb.
30	
31	That''s the Ad Hominem shape in answer-choice language: the argument goes after the "proponents" of a claim rather than the claim itself. When an argument answers a warning by talking about the people who gave it, look for this answer.'),
32	('6c54307e-858c-4e07-a8cc-87380a97345a'::uuid,'aff8cf0bfd81f87064661c69ed5b81ae','This describes circular reasoning, where the evidence already assumes the conclusion. The customer reports say coordination improved after drinking the juice. That wording doesn''t presuppose that the juice is safe or that there''s no reason to avoid it. The error is in how the herbalist handles the warning, not in how the evidence is phrased.'),
33	('78949977-e5e9-4cb9-8dab-2783c28b1b08'::uuid,'05f36bf48adb12fc8836c5872cdc27a2','The hippocampus is just one of the regions where theta waves occur. The argument never needs the hippocampus to cause anything; its leap is from theta waves to creativity. Delete the hippocampus from the stimulus and the flaw is still there.'),
34	('56f30592-2656-4219-9a42-0e7a63f26ae9'::uuid,'84066da687a95d44b1ef28cb67fe8cd9','Beck never weighs consistency against accuracy. Beck cares only about accuracy and uses consistency as evidence for it. The flaw is in treating consistency as proof of accuracy, not in ranking one above the other.'),
35	('ad3ae8af-3794-44f9-9116-44793695fe2e'::uuid,'de59ab3876de53cc98f8ab9ed2dad0b8','Beck''s conclusion is only about the accuracy of the program''s estimates of municipal automotive use. If the program were terrible at every other task, these estimates could still be accurate; if it were perfect elsewhere, these could still be wrong. Other tasks don''t bear on the conclusion.'),
36	('f93fb7ff-ccd7-4eab-9d10-7ee0723453fd'::uuid,'afdecc8cdd59cb088a57fd34d8708ce9','This is the trap, because it uses the right words in the wrong direction. Beck''s argument needs consistent output to come only from accurate estimates. C says Beck assumes the opposite: that the output would stay consistent even if the estimates were inaccurate.
37	
38	If Beck believed that, Beck''s own evidence would be useless, since consistency would then show nothing about accuracy. Consistent-but-inaccurate is the possibility Beck ignores, not something Beck takes for granted. That''s E.'),
39	('bb95918f-0553-4fd1-b61f-ff29aee09c6d'::uuid,'60d35056c5513c3629d52f00c0ef604f','Beck never judges the program''s overall value. The argument is only about whether its estimates are accurate. And even if accuracy were the only thing that mattered, the problem would remain: consistency still wouldn''t prove it.'),
40	('ab3c156d-0a6e-4b4d-b199-95b79f886e58'::uuid,'1b5fc370feca5283f32921187ff1fd9e','This names the scale that is always five pounds off. Beck treats consistency as enough for accuracy, and E points to the case that breaks that link: output that is consistent but inaccurate.
41	
42	Use the stem''s wording as a test. A "fails to consider X" answer is right when X, if true, would weaken the argument. If the program can be consistent and still wrong, Beck''s evidence no longer shows the estimates are accurate. That weakens it directly.'),
43	('0f248727-4894-4753-9003-dc641bf61838'::uuid,'05a9cb7625d830c657de08d38bebb1fb','This is the reversal in plain words. The premise says predators generally have short OPs. The conclusion needs the other direction: a mammal with a short OP is probably a predator. Those come apart whenever plenty of non-predators also have short OPs. If most short-OP mammals were not predators, Meg''s short OP would be weak evidence of predation.
44	
45	The move: when a premise says most X are Y, and the conclusion treats a Y as probably an X, look for the answer saying most Y might not be X.'),
46	('802e05ee-fea9-400e-9d9e-37363437f3e8'::uuid,'05ef6cfb9754a877b4c3dffc014360a0','The argument never needs attacking prey to be the only reason predators move their forelimbs fast. It needs a short OP to signal predation. Grant that predators also need speed for other things, and the gap is untouched: short-OP non-predators could still outnumber short-OP predators.'),
47	('1eff26af-34ed-4722-a4a0-d857e02c710d'::uuid,'6a71d7af9d6b26f58c652eb08f4724f5','Meg did have a short OP, so whether Meg could have been a predator without one doesn''t matter. C raises whether a short OP is necessary for predation. The argument''s problem runs the other way: it treats a short OP as enough to indicate predation.'),
48	('21687cc2-1a02-4190-842c-5b501d928818'::uuid,'768fcc711fc6c1ab70c7b0a3b76465ff','"Takes for granted" means ask whether the argument needs it. Negate it: suppose extinct mammals did have shorter OPs on average than living ones. That might make Meg''s short OP a bit less telling, but it doesn''t settle what the argument depends on: whether short-OP mammals were mostly predators. The argument never needs D, so D isn''t the flaw.'),
49	('c789ea93-0028-4030-a9e8-b04f45b49ab9'::uuid,'512ca44028398205320ed112bd87abfb','E is about what a longer OP is good for. Meg had a short one. Whatever advantage long OPs give, it tells us nothing about whether short-OP mammals are mostly predators. To hurt the argument, we''d need non-predators that also have short OPs, and E never gives us any.'),
50	('bcc78acd-7f8d-404a-a93f-a667edb16350'::uuid,'43fd4b6373e089e47d076f4e088cb172','This describes the correlation flaw: two things often show up together, so they must be connected. The columnist never points to two things occurring together. The evidence is one attitude (accepting a risk beforehand), used to doubt a different attitude (resentment afterward).'),
51	('6bcbd390-1bdb-4adc-8476-7ff19ecc5f3a'::uuid,'7d8ac678d2d39e066a366a396d322dc1','The columnist never relies on subjects judging the scientific value of placebos. The forms only show what risk subjects accept, and the conclusion is about how they feel. Give every subject a pharmacology degree and the gap between signing the form and living with the outcome is still there.'),
52	('a450dc87-4178-4eba-acc1-6f6b5b6ab32f'::uuid,'c3957a9d32ec86b95880b20504626ecf','The forms come from the very people the ethicists are talking about, test subjects, and "almost all" of them accept the risk. Nothing suggests a skewed slice. Even a perfect sample would still measure the wrong moment: the attitude before the outcome rather than after it.'),
53	('db4f9adf-253b-4dc4-b2e4-24b58a5bfe5e'::uuid,'49ca8dcc53bb166c9dec156d32e9ddbb','This is the timing gap. The forms capture attitudes before the test; resentment comes once the substance turns out to be ineffective. For the forms to cast doubt on resentment, the columnist has to assume attitudes don''t change in between.
54	
55	Negate it: suppose half or more of the subjects do change their attitudes once they learn their substance was ineffective. Then the forms tell us little about later resentment, and the argument falls apart. That''s what a "takes for granted" answer should name: an assumption whose negation breaks the argument.
56	
57	For next time: when evidence about one moment is used to settle a question about a later moment, check whether the argument assumes nothing changed in between.'),
58	('97638749-e69c-4650-892e-349370a7db9b'::uuid,'4b4ed2f803b670b72bb5927643e95aed','The conclusion isn''t moral. It doesn''t say subjects should or shouldn''t resent placebos; it doubts that many of them do. That''s a factual claim about how people feel, supported by factual evidence about what they signed. There''s no jump from facts to a moral verdict.')
59	) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;

1	insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values
2	('6e5e2f99-16fd-4746-94d4-54fbd32182f9'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Beck overlooks that the program could produce consistent figures that are still inaccurate. That''s the scale that is always five pounds off.
3	
4	Watch for the reversed wording. Beck assumes consistent figures could only come from accurate estimates. An answer saying Beck assumes the output would stay consistent even if inaccurate has it backwards.'),
5	('73c328e3-c57e-44eb-bba9-8421e3101d46'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not be predators. That''s the reversal in plain words: knowing most predators have short OPs doesn''t tell us most short-OP mammals are predators.
6	
7	Quick numbers. Say there are 10 predator species and 100 non-predator species. Every predator has a short OP, and so do 30 of the non-predators. Most predators have short OPs, but most short-OP mammals (30 of 40) are not predators. A short-OP mammal would then more likely be a non-predator than a predator, so Meg''s short OP wouldn''t make predation probable.'),
8	('e584e358-2f47-439a-a3db-cd92322c75ac'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the columnist takes for granted that subjects'' acceptance before the test holds up after they learn their substance was ineffective. Or the "fails to consider" version: subjects can accept a risk in advance and still resent it when it happens.')
9	) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;
