Ask which claim the second speaker''s objection would make false, then match that claim.'),\n('297d85c3-9a3c-4ce6-8cfc-b679b18393f1'::uuid,'f3c53bff609edebec1208102d3b30475','C asks whether evidence other than durability can establish a culture''s type conclusively. Robin never says durability is the only evidence, and Robin''s own claim is hedged with \"likely.\" Kendall never mentions other evidence at all. With neither speaker on record, there''s nothing to disagree about.'),\n('d83eee86-31b4-41da-8964-4fb4fd46f5a3'::uuid,'33662ddddafd89ee741e9170ad08b902','D brings in what \"many archaeologists have thought\" about the distinction''s importance. Neither speaker mentions other archaeologists or ranks how important the distinction is. Robin uses the distinction; Kendall questions one way of detecting it. Neither position is supported.'),\n('51760e4b-3637-4f26-ab1b-f94b788805c2'::uuid,'ab1915666a580c6588b207b891a06df2','Robin agrees: artifacts can reveal whether a culture was nomadic or sedentary. Does Kendall disagree? Kendall says artifacts reflect the materials available, which is itself something artifacts reveal. Kendall doubts one inference, not the value of studying artifacts. Rejecting one use of the evidence isn''t saying the evidence reveals little, so there''s no Disagree position.'),\n('5201a04c-8111-4353-93d9-b6599f6c1a52'::uuid,'aedcd5677b0cdd694452bc2effb6948a','Imagine two answers: twice the normal salt, or ten times. Either way, we still don''t know whether the rats had high blood pressure before the study. The amount of salt tells us how big the dose was, not whether the dose did anything.'),\n('cb25f9bc-5c54-402b-b304-cef5fa0449e6'::uuid,'b8294f555e5d4ec249d3bb6fbffc0c53','Suppose the high blood pressure caused serious harm, or none at all. The conclusion is only that high-salt diets are linked to high blood pressure. What high blood pressure does afterward can''t tell us whether salt produced it.'),\n('ea79abb1-e1f7-4376-b6f9-6235bf63eb65'::uuid,'e2b8ec31a54bf328f29b914afc454eea','How many wild colonies eat salty food tells us how common the diet is, not what it does. Whether the answer is 1 percent or 90 percent, this study''s evidence for a link is exactly as strong as before.'),\n('64884a51-d794-4886-84ae-ed3ef63e7e32'::uuid,'837d07997b4dadba38fa76ebfbd041c1','This is the before-measurement the study is missing. Run both answers. If most of the colony already had abnormally high blood pressure, the after-numbers show nothing about salt and the conclusion falls apart. If almost none did, a jump to 75 percent points straight at the diet.\n\nOne question, two answers, opposite effects on the conclusion: that''s what a relevant Evaluate question does. Next time a study reports only results after a treatment, ask what the numbers were before it.'),\n('834a2eeb-c931-4942-b72b-43979984981e'::uuid,'7f336a650ce87d814c2baeebbaa9a7ee','Yes or no, other rodent experiments say nothing about this colony. Even a yes wouldn''t tell us what those experiments found, and the conclusion is about rats anyway.'),\n('d0e37fc7-a4b8-4a25-b6d4-ac1d0ed28730'::uuid,'38ebfc79eb9abf751795db7d5d98a4dc','This is the appeal-to-fear flaw: accept my claim, or something bad will happen to you. The herbalist never threatens anyone with consequences for skipping the juice. If anyone raised a fear, it was the doctors, and the herbalist is the one brushing that fear aside.'),\n('e2ee319d-f723-40e0-9f20-44493b7aeb85'::uuid,'9c67ce0540b7fc3d8dda407410a41ba7','Check the premises against each other. Customers report better coordination; a few doctors say the herbs are potentially harmful. Both can be true at once: something can help your coordination and still carry a risk. No contradiction, so this flaw isn''t here.'),\n('ae200272-4dfb-4865-a572-40b509bbc532'::uuid,'eb3e2b89749cfce9014c7c30f45f325c','The doctors claim the herbs are potentially harmful. The herbalist never says why that''s wrong. Instead, the herbalist points out that doctors want a monopoly, then concludes there''s no reason not to try the juice. A motive to make a claim doesn''t show the claim is false. A doctor with a monopoly to protect can still be right about a harmful herb.\n\nThat''s the Ad Hominem shape in answer-choice language: the argument goes after the \"proponents\" of a claim rather than the claim itself. When an argument answers a warning by talking about the people who gave it, look for this answer.'),\n('6c54307e-858c-4e07-a8cc-87380a97345a'::uuid,'aff8cf0bfd81f87064661c69ed5b81ae','This describes circular reasoning, where the evidence already assumes the conclusion. The customer reports say coordination improved after drinking the juice. That wording doesn''t presuppose that the juice is safe or that there''s no reason to avoid it. The error is in how the herbalist handles the warning, not in how the evidence is phrased.'),\n('78949977-e5e9-4cb9-8dab-2783c28b1b08'::uuid,'05f36bf48adb12fc8836c5872cdc27a2','The hippocampus is just one of the regions where theta waves occur. The argument never needs the hippocampus to cause anything; its leap is from theta waves to creativity. Delete the hippocampus from the stimulus and the flaw is still there.'),\n('56f30592-2656-4219-9a42-0e7a63f26ae9'::uuid,'84066da687a95d44b1ef28cb67fe8cd9','Beck never weighs consistency against accuracy. Beck cares only about accuracy and uses consistency as evidence for it. The flaw is in treating consistency as proof of accuracy, not in ranking one above the other.'),\n('ad3ae8af-3794-44f9-9116-44793695fe2e'::uuid,'de59ab3876de53cc98f8ab9ed2dad0b8','Beck''s conclusion is only about the accuracy of the program''s estimates of municipal automotive use. If the program were terrible at every other task, these estimates could still be accurate; if it were perfect elsewhere, these could still be wrong. Other tasks don''t bear on the conclusion.'),\n('f93fb7ff-ccd7-4eab-9d10-7ee0723453fd'::uuid,'afdecc8cdd59cb088a57fd34d8708ce9','This is the trap, because it uses the right words in the wrong direction. Beck''s argument needs consistent output to come only from accurate estimates. C says Beck assumes the opposite: that the output would stay consistent even if the estimates were inaccurate.\n\nIf Beck believed that, Beck''s own evidence would be useless, since consistency would then show nothing about accuracy. Consistent-but-inaccurate is the possibility Beck ignores, not something Beck takes for granted. That''s E.'),\n('bb95918f-0553-4fd1-b61f-ff29aee09c6d'::uuid,'60d35056c5513c3629d52f00c0ef604f','Beck never judges the program''s overall value. The argument is only about whether its estimates are accurate. And even if accuracy were the only thing that mattered, the problem would remain: consistency still wouldn''t prove it.'),\n('ab3c156d-0a6e-4b4d-b199-95b79f886e58'::uuid,'1b5fc370feca5283f32921187ff1fd9e','This names the scale that is always five pounds off. Beck treats consistency as enough for accuracy, and E points to the case that breaks that link: output that is consistent but inaccurate.\n\nUse the stem''s wording as a test. A \"fails to consider X\" answer is right when X, if true, would weaken the argument. If the program can be consistent and still wrong, Beck''s evidence no longer shows the estimates are accurate. That weakens it directly.'),\n('0f248727-4894-4753-9003-dc641bf61838'::uuid,'05a9cb7625d830c657de08d38bebb1fb','This is the reversal in plain words. The premise says predators generally have short OPs. The conclusion needs the other direction: a mammal with a short OP is probably a predator. Those come apart whenever plenty of non-predators also have short OPs. If most short-OP mammals were not predators, Meg''s short OP would be weak evidence of predation.\n\nThe move: when a premise says most X are Y, and the conclusion treats a Y as probably an X, look for the answer saying most Y might not be X.'),\n('802e05ee-fea9-400e-9d9e-37363437f3e8'::uuid,'05ef6cfb9754a877b4c3dffc014360a0','The argument never needs attacking prey to be the only reason predators move their forelimbs fast. It needs a short OP to signal predation. Grant that predators also need speed for other things, and the gap is untouched: short-OP non-predators could still outnumber short-OP predators.'),\n('1eff26af-34ed-4722-a4a0-d857e02c710d'::uuid,'6a71d7af9d6b26f58c652eb08f4724f5','Meg did have a short OP, so whether Meg could have been a predator without one doesn''t matter. C raises whether a short OP is necessary for predation. The argument''s problem runs the other way: it treats a short OP as enough to indicate predation.'),\n('21687cc2-1a02-4190-842c-5b501d928818'::uuid,'768fcc711fc6c1ab70c7b0a3b76465ff','\"Takes for granted\" means ask whether the argument needs it. Negate it: suppose extinct mammals did have shorter OPs on average than living ones. That might make Meg''s short OP a bit less telling, but it doesn''t settle what the argument depends on: whether short-OP mammals were mostly predators. The argument never needs D, so D isn''t the flaw.'),\n('c789ea93-0028-4030-a9e8-b04f45b49ab9'::uuid,'512ca44028398205320ed112bd87abfb','E is about what a longer OP is good for. Meg had a short one. Whatever advantage long OPs give, it tells us nothing about whether short-OP mammals are mostly predators. To hurt the argument, we''d need non-predators that also have short OPs, and E never gives us any.'),\n('bcc78acd-7f8d-404a-a93f-a667edb16350'::uuid,'43fd4b6373e089e47d076f4e088cb172','This describes the correlation flaw: two things often show up together, so they must be connected. The columnist never points to two things occurring together. The evidence is one attitude (accepting a risk beforehand), used to doubt a different attitude (resentment afterward).'),\n('6bcbd390-1bdb-4adc-8476-7ff19ecc5f3a'::uuid,'7d8ac678d2d39e066a366a396d322dc1','The columnist never relies on subjects judging the scientific value of placebos. The forms only show what risk subjects accept, and the conclusion is about how they feel. Give every subject a pharmacology degree and the gap between signing the form and living with the outcome is still there.'),\n('a450dc87-4178-4eba-acc1-6f6b5b6ab32f'::uuid,'c3957a9d32ec86b95880b20504626ecf','The forms come from the very people the ethicists are talking about, test subjects, and \"almost all\" of them accept the risk. Nothing suggests a skewed slice. Even a perfect sample would still measure the wrong moment: the attitude before the outcome rather than after it.'),\n('db4f9adf-253b-4dc4-b2e4-24b58a5bfe5e'::uuid,'49ca8dcc53bb166c9dec156d32e9ddbb','This is the timing gap. The forms capture attitudes before the test; resentment comes once the substance turns out to be ineffective. For the forms to cast doubt on resentment, the columnist has to assume attitudes don''t change in between.\n\nNegate it: suppose half or more of the subjects do change their attitudes once they learn their substance was ineffective. Then the forms tell us little about later resentment, and the argument falls apart. That''s what a \"takes for granted\" answer should name: an assumption whose negation breaks the argument.\n\nFor next time: when evidence about one moment is used to settle a question about a later moment, check whether the argument assumes nothing changed in between.'),\n('97638749-e69c-4650-892e-349370a7db9b'::uuid,'4b4ed2f803b670b72bb5927643e95aed','The conclusion isn''t moral. It doesn''t say subjects should or shouldn''t resent placebos; it doubts that many of them do. That''s a factual claim about how people feel, supported by factual evidence about what they signed. There''s no jump from facts to a moral verdict.')\n) v(id,ob,nb) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;"}

{"project_id": "53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76", "sql": "insert into lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id,position,label,body) select v.e,v.p,v.l,v.b from (values\n('6e5e2f99-16fd-4746-94d4-54fbd32182f9'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying Beck overlooks that the program could produce consistent figures that are still inaccurate. That''s the scale that is always five pounds off.\n\nWatch for the reversed wording. Beck assumes consistent figures could only come from accurate estimates. An answer saying Beck assumes the output would stay consistent even if inaccurate has it backwards.'),\n('73c328e3-c57e-44eb-bba9-8421e3101d46'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the argument overlooks that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not be predators. That''s the reversal in plain words: knowing most predators have short OPs doesn''t tell us most short-OP mammals are predators.\n\nQuick numbers. Say there are 10 predator species and 100 non-predator species. Every predator has a short OP, and so do 30 of the non-predators. Most predators have short OPs, but most short-OP mammals (30 of 40) are not predators. A short-OP mammal would then more likely be a non-predator than a predator, so Meg''s short OP wouldn''t make predation probable.'),\n('e584e358-2f47-439a-a3db-cd92322c75ac'::uuid,3,'Goal','Look for an answer saying the columnist takes for granted that subjects'' acceptance before the test holds up after they learn their substance was ineffective. Or the \"fails to consider\" version: subjects can accept a risk in advance and still resent it when it happens.')\n) v(e,p,l,b) where not exists (select 1 from lr_explanation_steps x where x.explanation_id=v.e and x.position=v.p) returning id;"}

1	update lr_explanation_steps t set body=v.nb, label=case when v.nl='' then t.label else v.nl end from (values
2	('cd1342fe-bb3b-469c-a6a4-750b15d91103'::uuid,'833907d7a11daa688e3a71cf1a2e3b38','This isn''t a crazy argument. The author is assuming that what happened in the recent past will probably happen again in the near future.
3	
4	Given that September beat July in each of the last two years, how could we argue that July might still get more rain this year? Easily. Two years is a tiny record. Two seasons can line up that way by chance, the way a fair coin can land heads twice in a row. And maybe something about this year is different from the last two.
5	
6	Notice that the author was careful: the conclusion says "probably," not "definitely." That softer wording helps, but it doesn''t save the argument. Even "probably" needs more than two data points behind it. The flaw is the size of the record, not the strength of the claim.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
7	('f84a9bed-d622-4d14-9f81-6f71996ab903'::uuid,'59b658d134acad3a5d7513e71f1a073a','Look for an answer saying the prediction rests on too few past cases: two years is not enough to say what this year will do. An answer could also say the author overlooks that this year may differ in some important way from the previous two. Expect abstract wording, so be ready to translate "future event" and "instances of related past events" back into this year''s rain and the last two years.','Goal','Goal'),
8	('ab7e94a7-ddbd-4d45-9caf-4cb90af52647'::uuid,'f52e0550ab91b0ffcffe95dcaa682d50','There are exceptions to the general rule that people should practice what they preach. Rephrase: sometimes it''s fine for people not to act the way they tell others to act.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),
9	('d9b0a365-ab76-41ff-b451-3a338496b16a'::uuid,'38823ceea03e15ca139d751d4f48b4cc','Logicians don''t need to be logical in their discussions of logic, any more than physicians need healthy lifestyles in order to treat people.','Evidence (for instance)','Evidence (for instance)'),
10	('fc848127-25d4-473d-8483-fd17e81006ed'::uuid,'89eb980c119dabe5b2090f845b588692','The author''s example of an exception is an analogy. Physicians preach health, and nobody thinks a physician has to live a healthy life to treat patients well. So, says the author, logicians don''t have to be logical in their discussions of logic either.
11	
12	Any time an argument rests on an analogy, ask: are these two cases alike in the way that matters? The physician case works. A physician''s lifestyle happens outside the job. A physician who never exercises can still diagnose and prescribe correctly.
13	
14	The logician case is different. Being logical while discussing logic isn''t something a logician does after work. It is the work. A logician who argues illogically about logic is doing the job badly. How would anyone come away understanding logic if the lesson itself were illogical?
15	
16	So, given the physician example, how could we argue the logician example still fails? Point out that the physician''s failure is personal, while the logician''s failure is professional.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
17	('1a1ff027-bd4f-45ee-a3cb-3be320682ecd'::uuid,'11f10060dd9db32537ab5739863deb07','Look for an answer that breaks the analogy: being illogical in discussions of logic is a failure at the logician''s actual job, while an unhealthy lifestyle doesn''t stop a physician from treating people well. The stem says "fails to take into account," so the right answer will be a fact that, had the author considered it, would weaken the comparison.','Goal','Goal'),
18	('542f69a1-1945-43b7-8afc-9f50da21f625'::uuid,'6ff67bc1b8d98623183ee0476522d562','The professor said A''s would go only to papers whose conclusions were supported by reliable statistical evidence. My conclusions were supported by reliable statistical evidence, and the professor admits it, but my paper got a B.','Evidence','Evidence'),
19	('db12ab11-af52-46c2-a683-ab4d5b3b4340'::uuid,'d1d25a22e4ad401f5a96be602dbbd34c','When a Flaw stimulus contains conditional logic, suspect the most common famous flaw: Necessary vs. Sufficient. The author takes a rule and runs it backward or negates it.
20	
21	"Only" introduces a necessary condition. So the professor''s rule is:
22	
23	gets an A → conclusions supported by reliable statistical evidence
24	
25	That rule says reliable evidence is required for an A. It doesn''t say reliable evidence earns an A. The student reasons: my paper had reliable evidence, so it should have gotten an A. That is the reversal. It treats a requirement as a guarantee.
26	
27	An everyday version: "Only people with a ticket get into the concert." Having a ticket doesn''t promise entry. You could still be turned away for showing up after the doors close. The professor may have other requirements too, like clear writing or a sound argument, and the paper may have missed one of them. A B is perfectly consistent with the stated rule.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
28	('2aa2772e-075c-454e-93da-387d3cc3a000'::uuid,'3d151976f4c79fb256daeb8ead9934a7','Look for an answer calling out the formal Necessary vs. Sufficient error: the student treats something required for an A as if it were enough for an A. An answer could also say the student fails to consider that there may be other requirements for an A besides well-supported conclusions.','Goal','Goal'),
29	('6da4f1d9-4f19-4c6f-bd37-76f449cb2203'::uuid,'5e1295299b7e2142cda1461c773f261c','Lindsey is rebutting an opposing position. Some people say the company was unfair. Lindsey guesses at their reason ("Perhaps"): the company''s promise that the staff would all get bonuses if profits increased over last year''s. Then Lindsey knocks that reason down. The promise was:
30	
31	profits increase over last year''s → staff all get bonuses
32	
33	Profits were much smaller than last year''s, so the promise never kicked in, and it didn''t require bonuses. Fair enough. But Lindsey concludes much more: the company acted fairly.
34	
35	Given that the promise didn''t require bonuses, how could we argue the company still acted unfairly? Easily. Maybe the staff worked unpaid overtime all year. Maybe executives paid themselves large bonuses while the staff got nothing. The critics may have had reasons Lindsey never mentions.
36	
37	This is the famous Unproven vs. Proven False flaw. Defeating one argument for a claim doesn''t prove the claim false. Example: "Some people say this restaurant is bad, perhaps because the parking is terrible. But the lot was just doubled in size. Clearly the restaurant is good." The food could still be awful.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
38	('a6b9a3bb-235d-48b4-bc1a-ec837003f877'::uuid,'b536e4c1b8c0f443490307b4f4afdd81','Look for an answer saying Lindsey concludes a claim is false just because one possible reason for it fails. An answer might also point out that Lindsey only guesses at the critics'' reason ("Perhaps") rather than answering the reasons they actually gave.','Goal','Goal'),
39	('7ce366d3-9e04-4358-830c-cd3f66090c56'::uuid,'1e584ad4291dd745a84680eccb5c1d76','The recycling process for those products uses as much energy as producing them from raw materials, and almost all energy production damages the environment.','Evidence (and)','Evidence (and)'),
40	('f66b65fd-91c6-4001-820b-e9afc2bddf2d'::uuid,'9be55aeea7d4dc2f225f40008798e94b','Given that recycling these products uses as much energy as producing them from raw materials, how can we argue that making them from recycled materials is still better for the environment?
41	
42	One route would be to say the energy used for recycling is cleaner. The second premise mostly blocks that: almost all energy production damages the environment.
43	
44	So look past energy. Energy is only one way that making a product can harm the environment. What about mining, logging, or drilling for the raw materials in the first place? What about the waste that goes to a landfill when nothing is recycled? Recycling could win on those counts even if energy is a tie.
45	
46	The columnist treats one part of the environmental cost as if it were the whole cost. Two options that tie on one factor only tie overall if nothing else differs.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
47	('79ee2b71-105c-45d1-891a-006a162f1f84'::uuid,'f0274fb9d01aa6339ac4ec03781fc002','Look for an answer saying the columnist fails to consider environmental effects other than energy use. The best version would say recycling might help the environment in some way unrelated to energy. That would show the energy tie doesn''t settle the overall comparison.','Goal','Goal'),
48	('d90cc213-c178-450e-8472-21d25661714a'::uuid,'16f57f1dadfbafe56de2604f8f75a7ac','Six months ago the city raised the fines for parking violations, to help pay for a parking garage that had just opened. Since then, parking violations on city streets have dropped by 50 percent.','Evidence','Evidence'),
49	('a080ae89-c3a2-4236-8112-a1b0217ea8b3'::uuid,'dc3b8efad2eb1f477418c5c502a790f4','A common pattern in Logical Reasoning: "Policy X went into effect. Then Y happened. So X caused Y." The official assumes the higher fines caused the 50 percent drop. The conclusion depends on that causal story: "if we want there to be even fewer parking violations, the fines should be raised again" only makes sense if fines are what reduced violations.
50	
51	This is an Explain Curious Fact argument. Curious fact: violations dropped by half. The official''s explanation: the higher fines. Alternate explanation: read the first sentence again. A new parking garage opened at the same time. Drivers who used to park illegally on the street may now have somewhere legal to park. That would cut violations whether or not the fines changed.
52	
53	The official mentions the garage only as the reason the fines were raised, then forgets about it. That''s the detail the test wants us to catch. When a stimulus mentions a second change at the same time, ask whether that change could explain the result.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
54	('6a3f4f2e-bc5b-472f-b3f3-a54cd6a1c8e4'::uuid,'aa3ffdd3201c19a4c1788c0afd7e1217','Look for an answer saying the official fails to rule out another cause for the drop. The best one names the obvious rival: the new garage gave drivers more legal places to park.
55	
56	There''s also a smaller worry. Even if the fines did cause the drop, raising them again may not add much. At some point the fine is already high enough: whether it''s $500 or $600, I''m equally determined not to get that ticket. But the official only predicts "even fewer" violations, so this worry is weaker than the garage, which threatens the causal claim the whole recommendation rests on.','Goal','Goal'),
57	('838e1b73-7cfb-43f3-8a17-2fbf861c77b5'::uuid,'26da7a9b77023eda008d0c87d9018c9a','Let''s survey the customers at a sushi restaurant about whether they like fish. Or ask the people at a dog park whether dogs make good pets. We know what answer we''ll get.
58	
59	The president did the same thing. The survey went only to members present at a Tuesday meeting. Of course those people have no trouble attending on Tuesdays: they''re there. The members most likely to have a Tuesday conflict are the ones who missed the meeting, and they never got a survey.
60	
61	That matters because the attendance problem is about the people who don''t come. The president drew a conclusion about why members miss meetings from the members who didn''t miss this one. How many absent members stay home because Tuesday doesn''t work for them? The survey can''t tell us.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
62	('4a174753-dd5b-4207-a61e-681de2f49d5a'::uuid,'f72701dd92bd6b91eff9f0913feb5a06','Look for an answer describing a Sampling flaw: the president generalized from a sample that isn''t representative of the group the conclusion is about. The answer might say it abstractly (an unrepresentative sample) or concretely (the survey left out members who couldn''t attend on Tuesday).','Goal','Goal'),
63	('e4ba3f07-62ae-44d9-99f4-08896cab079b'::uuid,'8025116dfd8ab9dabd4288c943f75d6f','If the proposed measures are adopted, we''ll spend about a billion dollars less on ozone-related health costs.','Conclusion','Conclusion'),
64	('04d03232-ed43-4784-a704-d1c3dc7f2ce6'::uuid,'c5f2d914855f432f59613c67fc0ee7be','If the proposed measures are adopted, ozone levels in the city''s air will be one fifth lower than they are now.
65	Ozone is currently responsible for over $5 billion in health costs.','Evidence','Evidence'),
66	('700f2f4d-2273-44df-b221-4909c2471829'::uuid,'24fe13bc2051139990694452735d3fb7','The author takes one fifth of the $5 billion in ozone health costs and assumes that cutting ozone by one fifth cuts those costs by one fifth.
67	
68	But who said the relationship is one to one? Given that ozone will drop by a fifth, how could we argue the costs won''t drop by a billion?
69	
70	Maybe health costs depend on a threshold. Suppose ozone makes people sick only above a certain level, and even 80 percent of today''s ozone is well above it. Then cutting ozone by a fifth might barely change health costs. Or it could run the other way: the last fifth of the ozone could be the most harmful part, and the savings could be bigger than a billion.
71	
72	Either way, the argument needs costs to rise and fall in step with ozone levels, and it never shows that.','Evaluate','Evaluate'),
73	('06c62d20-4965-4cea-a503-b5cada434090'::uuid,'96b2c1b9250386a3ab970051f6dab7e8','Look for an answer saying "just because ozone is reduced by one fifth doesn''t mean ozone-related health costs will be reduced by one fifth." In flaw language: the author presumes, without evidence, that health costs are proportional to ozone levels.','Goal','Goal')
74	) v(id,ob,nb,ol,nl) where t.id=v.id and md5(t.body)=v.ob returning t.id;
